1.0 Introduction  
1.1 
Study Background and Purpose  
The City of Oceanside is updating the General Plan to establish a current long-range vision for the City and 
to plan for important community issues, such as new growth, housing and mobility needs, public services, 
and environmental protection. The Mobility Element – previously referred to as the Circulation Element – 
is one of the required “elements” or chapters that will go into the General Plan. 
 
This Existing Conditions Report is one of the initial steps in the Mobility Element planning process. It 
serves to document the current state of mobility within the City and identify needs. The report provides 
assessments of pedestrian, bicycle, transit, and vehicular mobility within the City of Oceanside. The 
analysis covers network connectivity, demand, quality, and safety for each travel mode. Additional topics 
covered in this report include existing intelligent transportation systems, transportation demand 
management techniques, as well as airport and goods movement. The Mobility Element 
recommendations will be informed by this Existing Conditions Report as well as recommendations from 
other General Plan elements, transportation modelling, community input, and direction from City staff 
and elected officials. 
 
The City’s current General Plan was adopted in 2002, while a focused update of the Circulation Element 
was performed in 2012. The 2012 Circulation Element identified the following challenges, opportunities, 
and transportation issues that are still relevant today: 
• 
Enhancing the City’s corridors for all modes of transportation 
• 
Increasing bicycle and pedestrian connections, routes, and facilities 
• 
Refining the City’s traffic calming program to promote safer streets for motorists, pedestrians, 
and bicyclists 
• 
Identifying and incorporating Intelligent Transportation System (ITS) technology for the City 
• 
Increasing support of Transportation Demand Management programs 
• 
Improving the efficiency of the existing transportation system 
 
Legislative Context 
Since the City’s last comprehensive General Plan Update, several key planning initiatives and state 
legislative actions have been adopted that redefine the way local transportation planning is carried out. 
Examples include Assembly Bill 1358 – Complete Streets Act, Senate Bill 375 – Sustainable Communities 
and Climate Protection Act, and Senate Bill 743 – Environmental Quality. 
 
In September of 2008, the State of California approved AB 1358 – the Complete Streets Act. Effective 
January 1, 2011, AB 1358 requires city or county legislative bodies to plan for a balanced, multi-modal 
transportation network that meets the needs of all users of streets, roads, and highways. “All users” is 
defined to include motorists, pedestrians, bicyclists, children, persons with disabilities, seniors, movers of 
commercial goods, and users of public transportation, in a manner that is suitable to the rural, suburban, 
or urban context of the general plan. 
 
SB 375 was also adopted in 2008, requiring metropolitan planning organizations (MPO) to formulate a 
“sustainable community strategy” (SCS) as part of their regional transportation plan (RTP). The SCS is 


Chapter 1: Introduction 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
Page 2 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
intended to identify how the region will achieve targeted reductions in greenhouse gas emissions from 
automobiles and light trucks through coordinated development patterns and transportation systems. 
Efforts at the regional level have embraced the sustainable and multimodal transportation emphasis 
directed by these legislative actions. The San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) is responsible 
for developing the San Diego region’s SCS. The SCS consists of the following five building blocks: 
•
A land use pattern that accommodates our region’s future employment and housing needs and
protects sensitive habitats and resource areas.
•
A transportation network of public transit, Managed Lanes and highways, local streets, bikeways,
and walkways built and maintained with reasonably expected funding.
•
Managing demands on the region’s transportation system (also known as Transportation Demand
Management, or TDM) in ways that reduce or eliminate traffic congestion during peak periods of
demand.
•
Managing region’s transportation system (also known as Transportation System Management, or
TSM) through measures that maximize the overall efficiency of the transportation network.
•
Innovative pricing policies, and other measures designed to reduce the number of miles people
travel in their vehicles, as well as traffic congestion during peak periods of demand.
SANDAG is currently underway with the 2021 Regional Plan which will be an update of the SCS and RTP. 
The 2021 Regional Plan is anticipated to reflect the new regional vision and further encourage sustainable 
development, including transportation infrastructure that supports future development patterns and 
helps to achieve regional greenhouse gas reduction goals set by the California Air Resources Board. The 
RTP will also address compliance with federal civil rights requirements (Title VI), environmental justice 
considerations, air quality conformity, and public participation. Addressing these requirements will ensure 
the region complies with federal laws and maintains eligibility for a variety of funding sources. 
The 5 Big Moves are at the heart of the 2021 Regional Plan vision. These key strategies envision a 
balanced transportation network that leverages technology to create safe, efficient, and socially equitable 
transportation system that increases access to opportunity and helps achieve environmental mandates. 
Aligning with the RTP vision will help position Oceanside to compete for grant funds and leverage regional 
investments. The 5 Big Moves consist of the following concepts: 
•
Complete Corridors provide a variety of travel choices and use technology to manage how
highways and major roads are used in real time. They provide a balance of dedicated, safe space
for everyone, including freight vehicles and people who walk, bike, drive, ride transit, and use
Flexible Fleets.
•
Transit Leap could create a complete network of fast, high-capacity, high-frequency transit
services that connect major residential areas with employment centers and attractions
throughout the San Diego region.
•
Mobility Hubs are places of connectivity where different travel options come together. They
provide an integrated suite of mobility services, amenities, and supporting technologies to better
connect high-frequency transit to an individual’s origin or destination.


 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 3 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
• 
Flexible Fleets build on the popularity of shared mobility services such as on-demand rideshare, 
bikeshare, and scootershare. These services can make it easier to connect to high-speed transit 
and other important destinations by providing a last-mile connection. 
• 
Next Operating System (OS) is a digital platform that uses technology and data to connect and 
manage different modes of transportation to improve overall efficiency and accessibility for 
people and goods to move throughout the region. 
 
SB 743 was signed into law in September 2013, making several changes to the California Environmental 
Quality Act (CEQA). This bill removes vehicular delay, level of service (LOS), parking and other vehicular 
capacity measures as metrics of transportation system impacts for mixed-use, infill or transit-oriented 
development projects. Vehicle miles traveled (VMT) is considered the new analysis metric used to 
measure transportation impacts. VMT reflects the automobile distance attributable to a project. The 
intent is to balance the needs of congestion management with statewide goals related to infill 
development, active transportation and transit use, and the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. 
Consistent with state mandates, the City of Oceanside adopted the Traffic Impact Analysis Guidelines for 
Vehicle Miles Traveled and Level of Service Assessment in August 2020. 
 
These guiding policies and documents emphasize the need for enhanced transportation networks that 
serve all travel modes, decrease reliance on single vehicle trips, and are closely tied to existing and 
planned development patterns. Building on these efforts, the current General Plan Update is a 
comprehensive process that will provide for consistent and integrated land use and transportation 
planning. The Mobility Element will identify goals, policies, programs, and infrastructure 
recommendations to improve multimodal mobility and accommodate anticipated growth. The Mobility 
Element will ensure the City is eligible and competitive for a variety of funding sources by complying with 
regional, state, and federal guidance and trends. 
 
Study Area 
Oceanside encompasses approximately 42 square miles and is home to a diverse population of more than 
175,000 residents. The Mobility Element study area consists of public transportation facilities within the 
City. A diverse and well-integrated transportation system serves the City, consisting of streets, regional 
freeways and highways, public transit services, pedestrian infrastructure, and bicycle facilities. 
 
Oceanside is situated in northwestern San Diego County, bordered by Marine Corps Base Camp 
Pendleton to the north, the City of Carlsbad to the south, the City of Vista and the unincorporated San 
Diego County community of Bonsall to the east, and the Pacific Ocean to the west. The coastal location 
defines the City’s western edge. Similarly, Marine Corps Base Camp Pendleton creates a firm edge to the 
north, with limited public connections. Figure 1-1 displays the City’s location within the region. 
 
The City’s transportation infrastructure, regional location, residential and employment density, and land 
use composition influence transportation mode choice for various trip purposes. Oceanside’s existing 
residential density (residential population per acre) is depicted in Figure 1-2. As shown, there are pockets 
of relatively higher residential population density spread across the City, including near multiple major 
transit stops: San Luis Rey Transit Center, Melrose Drive Station, El Camino Real Station, and Crouch 
Street Station.

Figure 1-3 presents employment density (jobs per acre). Like residential density, employment density 
concentrations vary across the City. Some of the greatest employment densities shown include: 
• 
West of I-5 surrounding Mission Avenue 
• 
The commercial/retail centers around Vista Way west of El Camino Real 
• 
North of Vista Way around Tri-City Hospital 
• 
The industrial park south of Oceanside Boulevard at the eastern part of the City 
 
Providing comfortable and convenient multimodal infrastructure within, and connecting to, higher 
residential and employment density areas helps achieve a balanced transportation system. These areas 
signify some of the greatest activity within the City, emphasizing the importance of safe and efficient 
transportation options. 
 
Commute mode shares for the City of Oceanside and San Diego County are compared in Figure 1-4. The 
City of Oceanside tracks similarly with the County as a whole. As is common in the United States, driving 
alone dominates the commute in both geographies, accounting for over 75% of all commute trips. 
Oceanside has less than half the share of walking and bicycling commuters, which may be in part due to 
the City’s regional location and distance from major job centers.  
 
Figure 1-4 
Commute Mode Share 
 
  Source: US Census, 2014 – 2018 American Community Survey 5-Year Estimates (2020) 
 
1.2 
Planning Process 
The identification of existing issues, needs, and opportunities will be further informed by input from 
community members and direction from City staff. Anticipated future land use alternatives will be used to 
forecast future traffic volumes. Collectively, this information will be used to identify transportation 
network alternatives intended to accommodate the anticipated growth while providing for enhanced 
mobility for each travel mode. The preferred transportation networks will then be derived through the 
analysis of future year conditions and additional community input.

The overall transportation network, policy, and program recommendations will be consolidated into the 
Mobility Element and unified with the other elements to form the General Plan. The new Mobility 
Element will provide a blueprint for Oceanside’s future development. 
  
1.3 
Document Review 
Reviewing adopted planning documents, studies and guiding policies greatly informs the planning 
process. The review helps build an understanding of existing issues and opportunities, while also 
identifying currently planned improvements. The Mobility Element is intended to compliment many of 
the foregoing planning efforts by incorporating and/or aligning with the recommendations and goals and 
policies previously set forth. This information will be referenced throughout the development of 
infrastructural and policy recommendations, helping to ensure feasibility and consistency with adopted 
guiding documents. 
 
The review covers the following City of Oceanside documents: 
• 
Climate Action Plan (2019)  
• 
Coast Highway Corridor Study (2019) 
• 
General Plan Circulation Element (2012)  
• 
Coast Highway Vision and Strategic Plan (2009)  
• 
Pedestrian Master Plan (2009)  
• 
Bicycle Master Plan (2008) and Bicycle Master Plan Update (2017) 
• 
A Vision for the Oceanside Boulevard Corridor (2007)  
• 
Nine-Block Pier Area Masterplan (2000) 
 
The following regional documents are also included: 
• 
Innovate 78 – Regional Profile (2019)  
• 
SANDAG’s San Diego Forward: The 2019 Federal Regional Transportation Plan (2019)   
• 
SANDAG Smart Growth Concept Map (2016) 
 
The full document review is provided as Appendix A. 
 
1.4 
Organization of the Report  
Following this introductory chapter, the remainder of this Existing Conditions Report is organized into the 
following chapters: 
• 
Chapter 2 Pedestrian Mobility describes current pedestrian conditions in terms of connectivity, 
demand, quality, and safety. 
• 
Chapter 3 Bicycle Mobility describes current bicycle conditions in terms of connectivity, demand, 
quality, and safety. 
• 
Chapter 4 Transit summarizes existing transit services, connections to transit, ridership, station 
and service quality, and pedestrian and bicycle safety near transit stations. 
• 
Chapter 5 Vehicular Mobility presents the existing street and freeway network, vehicular demand 
and operations, and safety.

City of Oceanside General Plan Update

• 
Chapter 6 Intelligent Transportation Systems identifies the existing technologies used to maximize 
transportation operational efficiencies in the City. 
• 
Chapter 7 Transportation Demand Management describes the current programs and efforts 
intended to encourage sustainable transportation behaviors, such as active transportation, 
rideshare, carpooling, and telecommuting. 
• 
Chapter 8 Airport and Goods Movement identifies the existing aviation connections and 
movement of goods throughout the City. 
• 
Chapter 9 Planning Issues and Implications summarizes the key findings for each travel mode. 
 
1.5 
Note on COVID-19 
This Existing Conditions Report was prepared in 2020 during the COVID-19 pandemic. Transportation 
services and travel patterns were some of the many aspects of daily life greatly impacted by this event. 
Due to the irregular travel patterns, analyses relied on historical count data and big data information to 
understand typical travel demands. Similarly, the review of existing public transportation services relied 
on those published prior to the pandemic to best depict typical conditions.  


 
Chapter 2: Pedestrian Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 10 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
2.0 Pedestrian Mobility 
This chapter summarizes the current documents and programmatic efforts related to pedestrian mobility. 
The existing pedestrian environment is described and assessed as it relates to connectivity, demand, 
quality, and safety. Providing adequate pedestrian access and quality infrastructure helps contribute 
towards a safe and comfortable walking environment. The degree to which people walk for 
transportation and leisure and their selected routes of travel are influenced by the comfort, safety, and 
convenience of their experience.  
 
Pedestrian comfort is influenced by separation from traffic, topography, the presence of sidewalks, and 
climate. Safety is determined by the speed and volume of vehicular traffic, crossing distances, traffic 
control, number of conflict points, and infrastructure design. Convenience is affected by distance and 
directness of travel. As connectivity increases, travel distances decrease.  
 
2.1 
Current Plans and Programs 
Pedestrian mobility in the City of Oceanside is largely guided by the Pedestrian Master Plan (2009), as 
well as the policies set forth in the current Circulation Element (2012). The City’s Safe Routes to School 
program and My Oceanside service request portal are two additional efforts that address pedestrian 
mobility and infrastructure. Each of these documents and programs are discussed in this section. 
 
The Pedestrian Master Plan established planning and design standards for infrastructure development 
that focuses on creating accessible and safe pedestrian networks. The intent of the Pedestrian Master 
Plan is to make walking a safer and more convenient mode of travel that is accessible by all. It identifies 
and prioritizes pedestrian projects resulting from technical analysis and community input and is used to 
support implementation and the City’s pursuit of grant funding. 
 
The Pedestrian Master Plan was driven by the following vision statement developed by the Plan’s working 
group, and input from city staff and the public: 
“In the future, Oceanside will see major improvements in its walking environment by 
making pedestrian facilities safer, connected, and accessible. All natural areas (including 
beaches, rivers and open spaces) will be well connected with urbanized areas where 
citizens live, learn, work, shop and play. Improved pedestrian environments will support 
policies such as smart growth, transit, and lower greenhouse gas emissions as well as 
healthy lifestyles. Pedestrian improvements will enhance neighborhood quality and 
promote walking as a practical, cost effective and attractive means of transportation that 
also supports tourism and commercial districts.” 
 
The Pedestrian Master Plan identifies seven goals addressing topics related to safety, walkability, 
connectivity, accessibility, alternative transportation, neighborhood quality, and funding. The goals, 
supported by objectives and policies, serve as focus topics to aid the City in achieving the established 
vision statement. 
 
The current Circulation Element acknowledges the importance of planning and developing multimodal 
transportation networks and the use of applying complete streets design concepts. Four of the five long-
term mobility goals outlined in the Circulation Element emphasize the importance of a multimodal 


 
Chapter 2: Pedestrian Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 11 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
transportation system that serve all users. The Circulation Element also incorporates the goals, objectives, 
policies, and top 25 infrastructure projects from the Pedestrian Master Plan. 
 
The City has a comprehensive Safe Routes to School project that will span the 2018/2019 through 
2020/2021 school years. The project seeks to create safer conditions around schools, and to encourage 
students, parents, and school staff to walk or bike to school. The effort involves school site assessments 
and walk audits at 26 elementary, middle, and high schools in Oceanside, as well as parent surveys and 
community events to promote the project. 
 
The maintenance of existing pedestrian 
infrastructure is important to providing safe 
pedestrian mobility and encouraging walking trips. 
My Oceanside is an online application-based service 
request portal where community members can 
identify issues and request services. Street 
maintenance is one of the categories available, 
where sidewalk or curb ramp related issues may be 
reported. The portal enables the user to identify the 
location, provide a description, and upload a photo. 
This tool helps community members take 
ownership of the issues they identify. 
 
2.2 
Pedestrian Network Connectivity 
Pedestrian infrastructure generally consists of sidewalks and pathways, crosswalks, and curb ramps. 
Pedestrian mobility is further supported by wayfinding signs, regulatory signs, street crossing features, 
and various amenities that all further contribute to pedestrian safety and comfort. Existing pedestrian 
infrastructure was inventoried to understand the existing network and identify locations with 
deficiencies. The inventory identified locations of sidewalks, curb ramps, and marked crosswalks. 
 
Figure 2-1 displays the locations of missing sidewalks along public roads except for the Fire Mountain and 
South Morro Hills communities where sidewalks are intentionally absent. Sidewalks are generally absent 
in the City’s older neighborhoods, such as South Oceanside, Loma Alta, and Henie Hills. These areas are 
predominantly single-family residential neighborhoods developed prior to standards requiring sidewalks.  
 
There are also sidewalks missing along some of the Mobility Element designated roadways, however, 
generally only along one side of the street without active adjacent land uses. The east side of Canyon 
Drive between SR-76 and Bush Street is an example of this, where no land uses are accessible, thus 
negating the need for a sidewalk. Additional examples include segments along El Camino Real, Oceanside 
Boulevard, and Vista Way. Constructing these sidewalks should be dependent on adjacent developments. 
 
Existing curb ramp locations are depicted in Figure 2-2. A total of 6,419 curb ramps were inventoried. 
Curb ramps play an important role in the pedestrian network. They are intended to support people using 
mobility assistance devices such as wheelchairs or walkers, and also improve the experience for people 
with strollers or other wheeled objects. Curb ramps also provide detectable warning surfaces intended to 
support individuals with visual impairments. These surfaces with raised physical indicators serve to alert 
pedestrians of a curb cut and street crossing.

Chapter 2: Pedestrian Mobility 
 Page 13 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 


 
Chapter 2: Pedestrian Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 14 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
American Disability Act (ADA) accessibility requirements are regularly reviewed and updated to ensure 
optimized designs, requiring public agencies to stay current when reviewing development projects and 
constructing new capital projects. Some of the curb ramp related topics covered by this include ramp 
running slope, ramp cross slope, landing/turning space slope and clearance, gutter/roadway counter 
slope, flush transitions, and roadway and sidewalk surface condition. 
 
Marked crosswalks are used to indicate preferred locations for pedestrians to cross. Crosswalks help alert 
motorists to anticipate and yield to pedestrians. Crosswalks are cited in locations where there is adequate 
visibility for approaching motorists and are convenient for the anticipated pedestrians. Figure 2-3 
illustrates different crosswalk markings as identified in the California Manual on Uniform Traffic Control 
Devices (CA MUTCD). In Oceanside, standard, ladder, and continental are the most common marking 
types, with the continental design being increasingly used. 
 
Figure 2-3 
Examples of Crosswalk Markings 
 
Source: 2014 CA MUTCD, Revision 5 
 
Marked crosswalk locations are shown in Figure 2-4, distinguishing by the number of legs marked. The 
majority of marked crosswalks are at signalized or stop-controlled intersections. Uncontrolled marked 
crosswalks are common in areas where of high pedestrian activity that may not satisfy a warrant for a 
traffic control. For example, Pacific Street has uncontrolled marked crosswalks to facilitate pedestrians 
crossing the street for beach access. The authority to establish marked crosswalks is covered through City 
of Oceanside Traffic Code Section 9. 
 
2.3 
Pedestrian Demand 
Pedestrian demand was evaluated through US Census data, an Active Transportation Propensity Model, 
and a review of data from the City’s network of automated pedestrian and bicycle counters. 
 
Commute mode share data was obtained from the US Census 2014 – 2018 American Community Survey 
5-Year Estimates for the City of Oceanside by Census Block Group. Figure 2-5 presents the estimated 
share of the working population that walks as their primary commute mode.

The concentration of walking commuters ranges from less than 1% to over 14%. The majority of the 
Census Block Groups shown have an estimated walking commute mode share of less than 1%, however, 
there are pockets of greater walking commute shares throughout the City. The greatest concentration of 
walking commuters is in the Downtown area, which consists of a variety of land uses and a strong street 
grid network that helps maximize connections for travel modes and reduces out of direction travel. 
 
The Active Transportation Propensity Model was used to identify areas with greater potential for walking 
and bicycling trips. The model combines two submodels: 
• 
Active Transportation Trip Generator Submodel – where trips originate or are generated from 
• 
Active Transportation Trip Attractor Submodel – where trips are going to or are attracted to 
 
Table 2-1 displays the inputs, thresholds, and multiplier values used to create the Active Transportation 
Trip Generator Submodel. A weight is assigned to each input, based on the relative influence the input 
has on generating active transportation trips. Generator input values listed as “high” reflect conditions 
with a greater likelihood of generating an active transportation trip, while those in the “low” range are 
understood to generate relatively fewer trips. The point value for the respective input category is 
multiplied by the weight value to assign a score for each input. 
 
Table 2-1 Active Transportation Trip Generator Submodel Inputs

 Weight

The greatest trip generator concentrations are shown in areas with mixtures of multifamily residential 
and employment-related land uses. Examples include: 
• 
The Downtown area 
• 
A portion of the Loma Alta neighborhood 
• 
Northwest of El Camino Real/SR-78 
 
The lowest concentration of active transportation trip generators is shown in the Morro Hills community, 
east of Vandergrift Boulevard and north of North River Road. 
 
The Active Transportation Trip Attractor Submodel was created using the input variables displayed in 
Table 2-2.  Each attractor is buffered by one-mile, with multipliers that decrease every quarter-mile 
interval away from the trip attractor. A point value is calculated by multiplying the distance multiplier and 
the weight assigned to each attractor. Land uses garner progressively lower weights in terms of their 
ability to attract active transportation trips as the distance required to travel along the roadway network 
to reach them increases.

Chapter 2: Pedestrian Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 21 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Additional pockets of trip attractors are shown along many of the Mobility Element designate roadways 
where retail land uses and bus stops are prevalent. In residential neighborhoods, the combination of 
schools and parks appear as trip attractors. The lowest concentrations of trip attractors are shown in the 
following areas: 
• 
Undeveloped parts of the City abutting Camp Pendleton 
• 
The Morro Hills area 
• 
Residential neighborhoods that are absent bus stops, parks, and schools 
 
Combining the trip generator and trip attractor submodels with equal weighting results in the Active 
Transportation Propensity Model shown in Figure 2-8. The areas exhibiting the greatest levels of 
propensity for active transportation trips include: 
• 
West of I-5 around Downtown 
• 
Western portion of Loma Alta 
• 
Southeastern parts of the Tri-City neighborhood 
• 
Northwest of the Melrose Drive Sprinter Station 
• 
Areas to the west and to the east of the San Luis Rey Transit Center 
 
These areas are representative of land use and demographic characteristics understood to be associated 
with relatively higher levels of active transportation trips. Realizing the full potential of this latent demand 
becomes more likely with the provisions of high-quality infrastructure and connections that make walking 
and bicycling trips more comfortable and convenient.  
 
The City of Oceanside maintains a network of permanent, automated counters that collect pedestrian 
and/or bicycle data 24 hours a day. One year of data was obtained (October 2019 – October 2020) from 
the three counters that collect pedestrian data. Median 24-hour counts were pulled from the data for 
weekdays and weekends to better understand pedestrian usage at these select locations. Pedestrian 
count locations and the median counts are shown in Figure 2-9. Count locations include: 
• 
San Luis Rey River Bike Path near College Boulevard 
• 
San Luis Rey River Bike Path near Pacific Street/Coastal Rail Trail 
• 
Coastal Rail Trail at the Oceanside Transit Center 
 
Activity was higher during the weekend periods for all three locations, with the greatest median 
estimates at the San Luis Rey River Bike Path near College Boulevard (342). 
 
2.4 
Pedestrian Environmental Quality Evaluation (PEQE) 
Pedestrian Environment Quality Evaluation (PEQE) assesses pedestrian conditions along roadway 
segments and crossings locations. The evaluation inputs for segments (sidewalks) and crossings are 
identified in Table 2-3. The results from each input are then summed to categorize each evaluated 
sidewalk and crossing leg as High, Medium, or Low, based on the following scale: 
High 
> 6 points 
Medium 
4 – 6 points 
Low 
< 4 points

Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
The PEQE study area was derived from the Active Transportation Propensity Model results depicted in 
Figure 2-8, by overlaying the high-scoring propensity areas with designated Mobility Element roads and 
SANDAG Smart Growth Opportunity Areas. Figure 2-10 displays the PEQE results. Table 2-4 summarizes 
the PEQE scoring results by sidewalk mileage and crossing leg. The PEQE scoring input tables are provided 
in Appendix B.  
 
As shown, pedestrian environmental quality along sidewalks east of I-5 were found to generally exhibit 
Low scoring conditions. These segments typically have higher speeds and lack vertical separation 
between the pedestrian realm and moving traffic. Conditions west of I-5 were predominantly scored as 
High or Medium. These areas typically featured wider sidewalks, lower posted speed limits, and vertical 
buffer separation (on-street parking). 
 
Table 2-4 Sidewalk Mileage and Intersection Crossing Legs by PEQE Rating

Source: CR Associates (2020) 
 
Nearly two-thirds (63.4%) of the crossing legs evaluated were found to exhibit Low scoring features. Low 
scoring crossings generally consist of standard crosswalks, curb ramps, and a traffic control. Crossing legs 
awarded medium scorings more commonly were high visibility crosswalks (continental crosswalks) with 
advance stop bars, and/or curb ramps with detectable warning surfaces. Locations receiving the High 
score exhibited additional features such as curb extensions, lead pedestrian intervals, and/or pedestrian 
signage. 
 
Many crossing locations could be improved from Low to Medium or Medium to High by implementing 
high visibility crosswalks, advance stop bars, and detectable warning surfaces on all curb ramps. These are 
low-cost treatments with demonstrated benefits to pedestrian safety and comfort. Additional features, 
such as enhanced pedestrian signage, curb extensions, and lead pedestrian intervals, should be used 
selectively higher levels of pedestrian activity are anticipated or to address potential sight distance issues.

2.5 
Pedestrian Safety 
Vehicle-pedestrian collision data was obtained from the Oceanside Police Department for the 5-year 
period 2015 – 2019. During the 5-year period, a total of 362 pedestrian-involved collisions were reported. 
Figure 2-11 displays pedestrian collisions by year. Total collisions were consistent throughout 2015, 2016 
and 2017, then experiencing a decrease in 2018 and 2019. 
  
Figure 2-11 Pedestrian Collisions by Year (2015 – 2019) 
 
Source: Oceanside Police Department (2020)  
 
Figure 2-12 displays pedestrian-involved collisions across the City. As shown, collisions are largely 
concentrated at intersection locations along major roadways, including Mission Avenue, Coast Highway, 
North River Road, and College Boulevard. A large cluster is prevalent along Mission Avenue west of Mesa 
Drive, and Coast Highway between SR-76 and Oceanside Boulevard. These locations align with the areas 
demonstrating the highest propensity for active transportation travel as previously depicted in Figure 2-8.  
 
Table 2-5 identifies the intersections with the most pedestrian collisions. Four of the nine leading collision 
locations were along Mission Avenue or Coast Highway, including the top two ranked locations. 
 
Table 2-5 Leading Pedestrian Collision Locations (2015 – 2019)

The ten violation codes attributed to the most pedestrian collisions are displayed in Table 2-6. Combined, 
these violations were assigned to 90% (324 of 362 collision records) of all pedestrian involved collisions. 
The violations indicate incidents of both pedestrians and motorists engaging in unsafe behaviors. 
 
The most common violation code cited, 21950(a), is due to drivers failing to yield to pedestrians when the 
pedestrian is crossing at a correct location. Addressing this may include treatments that define where 
drivers are to stop or yield, help alert drivers to anticipate pedestrians, reduce conflicts, give pedestrians 
priority, and improve pedestrian visibility to drivers. Examples of potential solutions may include:  
• 
High visibility crosswalks with advance stop bars 
• 
Enhanced signage 
• 
Right-turn on red restrictions at signalized intersections 
• 
Conversion of permissive left-turn signal phasing to protected left-turn phasing 
• 
Lead pedestrian intervals at signalized intersection 
 
The second leading violation code reported, 21954(a), involves pedestrians crossing at improper locations 
or times. Consideration may be given to implement additional marked crossings if they meet crosswalk 
warrants, including sufficient sight distance. Locations with high incidents of these violations, or high 
observations of mid-block crossings, may also be addressed through educational and/or enforcement 
programs, or traffic calming measures to reduce the potential of severe/fatal collisions.

3.0 Bicycle Mobility 
This chapter summarizes the current documents and programs related to bicycle mobility in Oceanside. 
Evaluations related to connectivity, demand, quality, and safety are used to summarize the current state 
of bicycling. Bicycling is an important consideration for mobility within a city. In recent years, bicycling has 
experienced a resurgence, both in terms of ridership and infrastructure planning and construction. As 
both commuter and recreational bicycle use continue to grow, bicycling is increasingly viewed as one or 
many potential solutions that can help address societal issues, such as traffic congestion, greenhouse gas 
emissions, public health, transportation costs, and built-out environments. 
 
Providing a safe, comfortable, and well-connected bicycle network helps to promote bicycling as a viable 
transportation option and recreational activity. In 2017, the City of Oceanside was awarded a Silver level 
Bicycle Friendly Community designation by the League of American Bicyclists. The City was previously 
awarded the Bronze level designation in 2008. This achievement is evidence of the City’s ongoing 
commitment towards improving bicycle mobility throughout the City.  
 
Four different types – or classifications – of bicycle facilities are recognized in California, including Class I 
bike paths (also known as multi-use path), Class II bike lanes, Class III bike routes, and Class IV separated 
bikeways (also known as protected bikeways or cycle tracks). Table 3-1 describes the typical 
characteristics of each bicycle facility classification. 
 
3.1 
Current Plans and Programs 
The City of Oceanside’s Bicycle Master Plan is the primary guiding document for bicycle mobility. The 
Bicycle Master Plan was last updated in 2017 and incorporated into the Circulation Element. The Bicycle 
Master Plan identifies project goals, infrastructure projects, and supporting programs. Infrastructure 
recommendations were prioritized by facility type. The plan also provides guidance related to facility 
design and funding sources. The following is a list of the three highest ranking projects for each facility 
type: 
 
Class I Bike Paths 
• 
Coastal Rail Trail – from Oceanside Boulevard to Morse Street, and from Vista Way to City 
Limit at Coast Highway 101/Carlsbad Boulevard 
• 
Oceanside-Escondido Inland Rail Trail 
• 
Rail Trail Connections to Guajome City Park 
 
Class II Bike Lanes 
• 
Old Grove Road between Ocean Ranch Boulevard and Mesa Road (eastbound) 
• 
El Camino Real between Vista Oceana and Mesa Drive (southbound) 
• 
El Camino Real between Mission Avenue and Douglass Drive  
 
Class III Bike Routes 
• 
Brooks Street/Division Street/Grant Street/Wisconsin Avenue between Mission 
Boulevard and Pacific Street 
• 
Plaza Drive and Thunder Road between College Boulevard and Lake Boulevard 
• 
Oceanside Boulevard between Coast Highway and Pacific Street

Table 3-1 Bicycle Facility Classifications 
 
Image 
Description 
Class I – Bike Path: Also referred to as a multi-use paths or 
shared-use paths, Class I facilities provide a completely 
separated right-of-way designed for the exclusive use of 
bicycles and pedestrians with crossflows by motorists 
minimized. Bike paths can provide connections where 
roadways are non-existent or unable to support bicycle 
travel. The minimum paved width for a two-way bike path is 
eight feet, with a two-foot wide graded area adjacent to the 
pavement. 
 
San Luis Rey River Trail pictured 
Class II – Bike Lane: Provides a striped lane designated for 
the exclusive or semi-exclusive use of bicycles with through 
travel by motor vehicles or pedestrians prohibited. Bike lanes 
are one-way facilities located on either side of a roadway. 
Pedestrian and motorist crossflows are permitted. Additional 
enhancements such as painted buffers and signage may be 
applied. The minimum bike lane width is five feet, or six feet 
when posted speeds are greater than 40 miles per hour. 
 
Oceanside Boulevard pictured 
Class III – Bike Route: Provides shared use of traffic lanes 
with cyclists and motor vehicles. Routes can be identified by 
signage and/or shared lane markings called “sharrows”. Bike 
routes are best suited for low-speed, low-volume roadways 
with an outside lane of 14 feet or greater. Bike routes provide 
network continuity or designate preferred routes through 
corridors with high demand. Traffic calming features such as 
speed humps, traffic circles, and curb extensions can be 
used to help keep traffic speeds and volumes low, enhancing 
the comfort along these shared use facilities. 
 
Pacific Street pictured 
Class IV – Separated Bikeway: Also referred to as 
protected bikeways or cycle tracks, Class IV facilities provide 
a right-of-way designated exclusively for bicycle travel within 
the roadway that is physically protected from vehicular traffic. 
Cycle tracks can provide for one- or two-way travel. 
Examples of physical separation include grade separation, 
flexible posts, railing, planters, or on-street parking. The 
minimum bike lane width is five feet, while the minimum 
separation width or buffer area is two feet.  
 
Leucadia Boulevard in Encinitas pictured

In addition to the Bicycle Master Plan goals, the Circulation Element identifies the following two goals, 
supported by a series of objectives and policies: 
Goal 1: Provide a safe, interconnected network of bicycle facilities within Oceanside for 
recreational and commuter users. 
Goal 2: Make bicycling a viable mode choice in an effort to reduce congestion, improve air 
quality, and provide residents and visitors with public health and recreational benefits. 
 
The San Diego Regional Bicycle Plan identifies regional corridors to comprise the regional bicycle network. 
The corridors represent vital bicycle connections to local and regional activity centers and transit facilities 
that serves the needs of people using their bicycle for transportation and recreational trips. Five corridors 
are identified within Oceanside, including: 
• 
Camp Pendleton Trail (Class III Bike Route) 
• 
Coastal Rail Trail (Class I Bike Path & Class II Bike Lanes) 
• 
El Camino Real (Class II Bike Lanes), Inland Rail Trail (Class I Bike Path) 
• 
San Luis Rey River Trail (Class I Bike Path) 
• 
Inland Rail Trail (Class I Bike Path) 
 
In 2013, the SANDAG Board of Directors approved the Regional Bike Plan Early Action Program (EAP), 
committing $200 million to expand the bike network countywide and finish high-priority projects within a 
decade. Within Oceanside, the Coastal Rail Trail segment between Wisconsin Avenue and Oceanside 
Boulevard was funded through the construction phase and has since been completed. Additional facilities 
prioritized yet falling outside of the funding allocation include the following: 
• 
Coastal Rail Trail along Broadway and Eaton Street 
• 
Coastal Rail Trail from Oceanside Boulevard to south of Buccaneer Park 
• 
Inland Rail Trail along the Sprinter line  
 
A variety of programmatic offerings are available to further encourage safe bicycling behaviors at the 
local and regional levels. The Oceanside Bicycle Map is a pocket sized map that provides valuable 
information for bicyclists of all abilities. The map identifies existing bicycle facilities, including access 
locations to the Coastal Rail Trail and San Luis Rey River Trail. The map also identifies the location of 
bicycle shops, recreation centers, and skate parks. To help bicyclists traverse Camp Pendleton, the map 
explains the process for bicyclists to obtain a permit to enter the base and by-pass Interstate 5 with 
supporting links. The back of the map provides information related to safe cycling as well as California 
state laws pertaining to bicyclists. 
 
The Oceanside Bicycle and Pedestrian Committee, also referred to as BikeWalk Oceanside, is an advisory 
committee that serves to promote bicycling and walking. The group holds public meetings the third 
Monday of each month at 9:00AM in the Library Committee Room. BikeWalk Oceanside regularly offers 
bike safety assemblies and bike rodeos at elementary schools. The City of Oceanside provides funding for 
adult bicycle education classes every other month at no cost to participants, taught by League of 
American Cyclist Certified Instructors. The Traffic School 101 course includes three hours of classroom 
instruction and six hours of on road skill instruction. The City of Oceanside is the only municipality in San 
Diego County that provides funding for adult and elementary education on a regular basis. The City also 
provides information regarding safe bicycling behaviors and considerations for motorists. The Bicycles – 
Vehicles Share the Road fact sheet describes three common bicycle facility classifications (bicycle path, 


 
Chapter 3: Bicycle Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 33 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
bicycle lane, and bicycle routes), and emphasizes the responsibilities and requirements for riding a 
bicycle. Through SANDAG’s iCommute program, employers can offer adult bicycle education programs to 
their staff without charge. The classes available include bicycle safety checks, a bike class, and a Bike and 
Learn Together class which teaches on-road and commuting skills. 
 
The City of Oceanside regularly participates in the regional Bike to Work Day organized by SANDAG. Each 
year in May, people are encouraged to make their work or school commute via bicycle and are invited to 
stop by pit stops throughout the region for beverages, snacks, t-shirts, and other incentives. In 2019, the 
City of Oceanside co-hosted two pit stops, including one at Civic Center Plaza and Mance Buchanon Park 
on the San Luis Rey River Trail. 
 
3.2 
Bicycle Network Connectivity 
Figure 3-1 displays the City’s existing bicycle facilities. Table 3-2 identifies the existing bicycle mileage by 
classification. Oceanside’s bicycle network is currently comprised of Class I, II, and III facilities, with bike 
lanes making up nearly 70% of the network. 
 
Table 3-2 Existing Bicycle Network Mileage by Classification 
Classification 
Existing Mileage 
Class I Bike Path 
11.6 
Class II Bike Lanes 
72.7 
Class III Bike Route 
21.7 
Class IV Separated Bikeway 
0.0 
Total Network Mileage 
106.0 
Source: CR Associates (2020) 
 
The network provides great coverage throughout the City with facilities interconnected to one another. 
All Sprinter stations, the Oceanside Transit Center, and San Luis Rey Transit Center can be accessed via 
bike paths or bike lanes. East of I-5, the development patterns have resulted in largely segregated land 
uses. The City has many large single-family residential neighborhoods, while commercial uses are largely 
concentrated along arterials. This potentially results in long distances for commute or shopping trips but 
may benefit recreational riding internal to neighborhoods and to destinations like local parks or 
elementary schools. The natural topography presents another challenge for cyclists, particularly in the 
north-south directions. The hills and valleys combined with high speed arterials may act as deterrents. 
 
In the older communities west of El Camino Real, Class III bike routes are the primary facility due to more 
constrained roadways and built out environments. Many of these roadways have lower posted speed 
limits and low levels of traffic volumes, making bike routes an appropriate facility type. Traffic calming 
features, such as curb extensions, traffic circles, and speed humps can be deployed along these routes to 
facilitate slower traffic speeds and improve the comfort and safety of bicyclists. 
 
Bike lanes largely comprise the network east of El Camino Real where the development patterns include 
wider roads. Many of the arterials with bike lanes have high posted speed limits that may result in 
uncomfortable conditions for cyclists. Examples include El Camino Real, College Boulevard, Oceanside 
Boulevard, and Rancho Del Oro.

Chapter 3: Bicycle Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 35 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
The existing Class I facilities are components of the regional San Luis Rey River Trail and Coastal Rail Trail. 
The San Luis Rey River Trail is a Class I bike path that provides an east-west connection completely 
separated from vehicular traffic from the coast to just east of College Boulevard. The western terminus of 
the San Luis Rey River Trail connects to a segment of the Coastal Rail Trail. When complete, this planned 
44-mile regional Class I bike path will run parallel to the rail corridor for the full length of Oceanside and 
other coastal cities, eventually terminating at Santa Fe Depot in Downtown San Diego. 
 
Bicycle parking is an additional component of the bicycle network. Bicycle parking can be categorized as 
intended for short term or long term parking. Short term parking is intended for two hours or less and 
generally consists of single or multiple bike racks that can come in a variety of forms. Long term parking is 
intended for periods greater than two hours and may serve commuters, employees, students, or 
residents. The City of Oceanside’s Bicycle Master Plan includes guidance on bicycle parking siting and 
design considerations. 
 
Bike racks are common at shopping centers and schools throughout Oceanside. Additionally, the City has 
installed over 50 bike racks throughout the Downtown area. An enclosed and secured bike parking 
structure is located at the Oceanside Transit Center. The City Hall BikeStation also provides secure bike 
parking options at City Hall, available for all City employees and Downtown workers. Bike lockers are also 
present at all of the Sprinter Stations in the City. 
 
Bikes are also accommodated on public transportation services. North County Transit District buses can 
accommodate two-three bikes. Rail services such as the Sprinter, Coaster, Metrolink, and Amtrak also 
have provisions for bikes. 
 
3.3 
Bicycle Demand 
Bicycle demand was evaluated through US Census data, the Active Transportation Propensity Model 
presented in the Pedestrian Demand section, and a review of data from the City’s network of automated 
pedestrian and bicycle counters. 
 
Commute mode share data was obtained from the US Census 2014 – 2018 American Community Survey 
5-Year Estimates for the City of Oceanside by Census Block Group. Figure 3-2 presents the estimated 
share of the working population that bikes as their primary commute mode. The greatest concentration 
of bicycle commuters is shown in the area just south of the Mission Avenue / Mesa Drive intersection, 
which also has some of the City’s greatest employment density (Figure 2-2). Additional areas with 
relatively higher levels of bicycle commuters are shown west of I-5 south of Oceanside Boulevard and 
near the Oceanside Transit Center. These areas are connected by multiple bicycle facilities and are near 
job concentrations, with characteristics that may contribute to the higher commute rates. 
 
The arterials near areas with higher propensity for active transportation trips are generally well-served by 
existing bicycle facilities. The importance of completing the Coastal Rail Trail is emphasized by the high 
concentration of active transportation trip generators and attractors throughout the Downtown area. 
Similarly, pockets of moderately high active transportation propensity are located near several Sprinter 
stations, which are adjacent to the planned Inland Rail Trail. Completing these projects will provide a 
high-quality links to Oceanside’s bicycle network, that are comfortable for all users and access major 
activity centers.

Median 24-hour counts for weekdays and weekends were developed for each of the City’s six automated 
bicycle count stations. Bicycle count locations and the median counts are shown in Figure 3-3. Three of 
the stations are along the Class I paths where the pedestrian counters are located, with the remaining 
three bicycle counters along Class II bike lanes, including: 
• 
Northbound Coast Highway near the southern City boundary 
• 
Northbound Coast Highway near Eaton Street 
• 
Eastbound Oceanside Boulevard near Rancho Del Oro Drive 
 
Activity was much greater during the weekend periods for all locations except Oceanside Boulevard and 
the Coastal Rail Trail locations. These locations may capture greater commute use due to the proximity to 
the Oceanside Transit Center and Sprinter Stations, whereas the other locations likely exhibit greater 
recreational use. The greatest median bicycle volume was estimated along the San Luis Rey River Bike 
Path near Pacific Street/Coastal Rail (979), followed by the San Luis Rey River Bike Path near College 
Boulevard (882). 
 
3.4 
Bicycle Facility Quality 
Bicycle facility quality was assessed using the bicycle Level of Traffic Stress (LTS) methodology, as 
developed by Mekuria, et al. (2012) of the Mineta Transportation Institute and reported in Low-Stress 
Bicycling and Network Connectivity. LTS classifies the street network into categories according to the level 
of stress it causes cyclists. Factors influencing the score include: 
• 
Cyclist’s physical separation from vehicular traffic 
• 
Vehicular traffic speeds along the roadway segment 
• 
Number of travel lanes 
• 
Factors related to intersection approaches with dedicated right-turn lanes and uncontrolled 
crossings 
 
Table 3-3 identifies the four LTS categories and provides a description of the traffic stress experienced by 
the cyclist and the environmental characteristics consistent with the category. LTS scores range from 1 
(lowest stress) to 4 (highest stress) and correspond to roadways that different populations may find 
suitable for riding on, considering their stress tolerance. The populations identified in the table are drawn 
from a classification system developed by City of Portland Bicycle Coordinator Roger Geller to generally 
categorize the population into one of four types of cyclists: 
• 
Strong and Fearless: People willing to bicycle with limited or no bicycle-specific infrastructure 
(estimated 1% of the population) 
• 
Enthused and Confident: People willing to bicycle if some bicycle-specific infrastructure is in place 
(estimated 7% of the population) 
• 
Interested but Concerned: People willing to bicycle if high-quality bicycle infrastructure is in place 
(estimated 60% of the population) 
• 
No Way, No How: People unwilling to bicycle even if high-quality bicycle infrastructure is in place 
(estimated 37% of the population

Figure 3-4 shows the LTS results for all public roadways and paths in the City, excluding freeways. Table 3-
4 summarizes the scores. Over 80% of bikeable roadways exhibit LTS 1 or 2 characteristics, meaning they 
are comfortable for most levels of cyclists, while approximately 15% of the network was scored as LTS 4. 
The LTS 1 or 2 roadways are largely located within residential neighborhoods, while the surrounding 
arterials tend to exhibit LTS 3 or 4 conditions, making it difficult for cyclists to ride between 
neighborhoods or to most destinations under low stress conditions. 
 
West of I-5 is an exception, where the street grid network provides many parallel alternative route 
options. For example, Mission Avenue crossing I-5 was scored as LTS 4, however, LTS 1 or 2 crossings are 
available just to the north at Civic Center Drive/Bush Street and to the south at Division Street/Country 
Club Lane. All Class I segments are automatically scored as LTS 1, as they are completely separated from 
vehicles.

3.5 
Bicycle Safety 
Vehicle-bicycle collision data was obtained from the Oceanside Police Department for the 5-year period 
2015 – 2019. During the 5-year period, a total of 202 bicycle-involved collisions were reported. Figure 3-5 
displays bicycle collisions by year. Annual collisions peaked in 2018 with 50 collisions, while the other 
years closely ranged between 36 – 40 collisions per year. 
  
Figure 3-5 
Bicycle Collisions by Year (2015 – 2019) 
 
Source: Oceanside Police Department (2020)  
 
Figure 3-6 displays bicycle-involved collisions across the City, while Table 3-5 identifies the individual 
locations with the greatest number of bicycle collisions. As shown, collisions are largely concentrated 
west of I-5, along Mission Avenue and Coast Highway, where the propensity model indicated high 
potential for active transportation demand. Additional collision concentrations are shown along Harbor 
Drive, College Boulevard near Oceanside Boulevard, Oceanside Boulevard near I-5, Rancho Del Oro north 
of Mesa Drive, intersections along SR-76, and Mission Avenue throughout the valley.

Table 3-6 identifies the ten most frequent violation codes attributed to bicycle collisions. Collision fault 
information was not provided within the data, however, multiple violations definitions indicate instances 
where bicyclists were riding against traffic or improperly positioned within the roadway. Unsafe 
movements, unsafe speeds, and failure to yield the right-of-way were other common violations.  
 
Continuing the educational offerings to inform bicyclists about safe riding behaviors and raising driver 
awareness to better anticipate the presence of cyclists may help encourage safe riding and driving. 
Similarly, the provision of high-quality infrastructure dedicated to bicyclists can encourage cyclists to 
operate within designated spaces and better avoid conflicts with motor vehicles. 
 
Table 3-6 Leading Bicycle Collision Violation Codes (2015 – 2019) 
 
Violation 
Code 
Description 
Number of 
Collisions 
MISC-HAZ 
Miscellaneous Hazard 
33 
22107 
No person shall turn a vehicle from a direct course or move right or left upon a roadway 
until such movement can be made with reasonable safety… 
25 
21804 
The driver of any vehicle about to enter or cross a highway from any public or private property, 
or from an alley, shall yield the right-of-way to all traffic, as defined in Section 620, 
approaching on the highway close enough to constitute an immediate hazard, and shall 
continue to yield the right-of-way to that traffic until he or she can proceed with reasonable 
safety. 
15 
22350 
No person shall drive a vehicle upon a highway at a speed greater than is reasonable or 
prudent having due regard for weather, visibility, the traffic on, and the surface and width of, 
the highway, and in no event at a speed which endangers the safety of persons or property. 
15 
21650.1 
A bicycle operated on a roadway, or the shoulder of a highway, shall be operated in the same 
direction as vehicles are required to be driven upon the roadway. 
14 
21801(a) 
The driver of a vehicle intending to turn to the left or to complete a U-turn upon a highway, or 
to turn left into public or private property, or an alley, shall yield the right-of-way to all vehicles 
approaching from the opposite direction which are close enough to constitute a hazard at any 
time during the turning movement, and shall continue to yield the right-of-way to the 
approaching vehicles until the left turn or U-turn can be made with reasonable safety. 
13 
21453(a) 
A driver facing a steady circular red signal alone shall stop at a marked limit line, but if none, 
before entering the crosswalk on the near side of the intersection or, if none, then before 
entering the intersection, and shall remain stopped until an indication to proceed is shown, 
except as provided in subdivision (b). 
10 
21658(a) 
A vehicle shall be driven as nearly as practical entirely within a single lane and shall not be 
moved from the lane until such movement can be made with reasonable safety. 
6 
21650 
Upon all highways, a vehicle shall be driven upon the right half of the roadway…

21208(a) 
Whenever a bicycle lane has been established on a roadway pursuant to Section 21207, any 
person operating a bicycle upon the roadway at a speed less than the normal speed of traffic 
moving in the same direction at that time shall ride within the bicycle lane…

Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
4.0 Transit 
This chapter identifies the existing public transportation routes and stops, ridership and demand, as well 
as quality of transit stops and services. Pedestrian and bicycle collision data near transit stops is also 
presented, considering many people walk or ride a bicycle to access transit stops. 
 
Public transportation can provide many societal benefits, such as improved mobility options, reduced 
roadway congestion, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions. To realize these benefits, many variables 
must align. Transit offerings must be efficient and well-connected, serve trip origins and key destinations, 
and be dependable. Transit planning is a coordinated effort due to its regional importance, involving local 
jurisdictions, SANDAG and the North County Transit District (NCTD). 
 
The currently adopted Circulation Element identifies two transit-specific goals for the City, supported by 
objectives and policies. The goals read as follows: 
• 
Support the increased use and availability of transit and rail service to encourage a multimodal 
transportation network in Oceanside. 
• 
Ensure that Oceanside residents have adequate and convenient public transportation by 
collaborating with NCTD. 
 
The City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan (CAP) (2019) recognizes the important role public transit can 
play towards reducing greenhouse gas emissions. A variety of strategies identified in the CAP are based 
around transit, including encouraging smart growth development concentrated near public transit stops, 
a transit subsidy program, improvements to transit stop amenities, and enhanced walking and biking 
connections to transit stops. These strategies will help improve access to transit services and improve the 
comfort of transit riders which further encourages use. Focusing development near transit stops may 
increase the viability of transit as a commute mode for future community members. 
 
The Oceanside Transit Center serves as a major regional transit hub, with Breeze Bus, Coaster, Sprinter, 
Amtrak, and Metrolink services all offered. It is the only location in the region where all of these services 
converge. The Oceanside Transit Center is the northern terminus of the Coaster, western terminus of the 
Sprinter, and southern terminus of the Metrolink. In early 2020, NCTD issued a request for proposals 
from developers to redevelop the Oceanside Transit Center into a mixed-use, transit-oriented 
development site with improved intermodal connectivity. The project also seeks to improve the interface 
with the Downtown area and increase activation of the area. The transit center is in one of the highest 
active transportation propensity areas in the City, and the redevelopment has the potential to facilitate 
further demand for transit use, walking, and bicycling within this high activity area. 
 
4.1 
Transit Routes and Stops 
Figure 4-1 displays the existing public transportation routes and stops within Oceanside. A 0.25-mile 
walkshed is also depicted around each transit stop. Most neighborhoods have transit options running 
along arterials surrounding the periphery. Transit is less accessible in neighborhoods with lower 
residential density, such as Morro Hills, Fire Mountain, Henie Hills, Capistrano north of SR-76, and north 
of the airport. The remainder of this subsection provides individual route maps, route descriptions, and 
service frequency for each transit route within the City.

Bus Service 
Bus Route 101 
Route 101 operates between the Oceanside Transit Center 
and the VA, UCSD, and UTC via Highway 101. Aside from 
the Transit Center, there are no stops in the City of 
Oceanside. It runs on approximately 30 minutes headways 
during weekdays and weekends.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bus Route 302 
Route 302 operates between the Oceanside Transit Center and the Vista Transit Center. The route follows 
the South Coast Highway along the coast before turning inland at Vista Way. Bus stops are concentrated 
on the South Coast Highway, Vista Way and Rancho Del Oro. Headways are approximately 15 minutes on 
weekdays and 30 minutes on weekends. 
 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 48 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Bus Route 303 
Route 303 is operated from the Oceanside Transit Center to the Vista Transit Center. The route has stops 
along Mission Avenue, North River Road, and North Santa Fe Avenue. The route operates at 15-minute 
headways on weekdays and weekends. 
 
 
 
 
 
Bus Route 309 
Bus Route 309 travels from the San Luis Rey Transit Center, 
on Mission Avenue and El Camino Real via the Plaza Camino 
Real Transit Center and south to Encinitas Station. For 
selected trips, the route follows Douglas Avenue. Route 309 
runs on headways of 30-minutes throughout the week.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 49 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Bus Route 311 
Route 311 operates from the San Luis Rey Transit Center to 
Rancho Del Oro SPRINTER Station via Vandegrift Boulevard, 
Douglas Drive, Mission Avenue, Via Rancho Road, Ocean 
Ranch Boulevard, and Oceanside Boulevard. Monday through 
Friday, the route operates at 20- to 30-minute highways 
during morning peak hours and one-hour headways during 
evening peak hours. It does not operate on weekends. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bus Route 313 
Route 313 runs from the Oceanside Transit Center to the San Luis Rey Transit Center in the City of 
Oceanside via Mesa Drive, Rancho Del Oro Drive, Frazee Road, and College Boulevard. The Route 
operates on a one-hour headway Monday through Friday and does not operate on the weekends or on 
holidays. 
 
 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 50 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Bus Routes 315 and 325 
Route 315 runs from Carlsbad Village Station to 14 Area in 
Camp Pendleton. The route follows Vista Way, Thunder Drive, 
College Boulevard, and Vandegrift Boulevard. Route 325 is 
primarily along the same path but terminates at the College 
Boulevard SPRINTER Station. Route 315 operates at 60-minute 
headways Monday through Friday. For areas served by Route 
325 headways are improved to 30 minutes. Route 315 
operates at 60-minute headways on Saturday with limited 
supplemental service on Route 325. On Sundays, Route 315 
has 60-minute headways and Route 325 does not operate. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bus Route 318 
Route 318 runs from the Oceanside Transit Center to the Vista Transit Center via the South Coast 
Highway, Oceanside Boulevard, Bobier Drive, and Vista Way. Throughout the week, the route operates at 
one-hour headways. 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 51 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Bus Route 323 
Bus Route 323 primarily travels from the College Boulevard 
SPRINTER Station in Oceanside around a loop connecting 
Oceanside Boulevard, Rancho Del Oro Drive, Mesa Drive, Old 
Grove Road, and Avenida del Oro. The route then continues 
south along Emerald Drive and Lake Boulevard, terminating at 
Quarry Creek. This section operates at one-hour headways 
Monday through Friday with no operations on weekends or 
holidays. The route extends to Plaza Camino Real Transit 
Center, Carlsbad High School, and Sage Creek High School in 
Carlsbad twice in the morning, with one-hour headways, and 
once in the afternoon. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bus Route 332 
Bus Route 332 is predominantly operated in the City of Vista. It follows Melrose Drive and Sycamore 
Avenue. Roughly a quarter mile of the route and three bus stops are within the City of Oceanside on 
Melrose Drive. On weekdays, during the morning peak period, the route is operated at 10 to 15-minute 
headways; for the remainder of the day, it is operated at 30-minute headways. On Saturday and Sunday 
the route is operated at 90-minute headways. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 52 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
FLEX Bus Route 392 
NCTD operates FLEX Bus Route 392 from Oceanside Transit 
Center north along Vandegrift Boulevard to 14 Area at Camp 
Pendleton. This route has a set schedule with approximately 
one-hour headways on weekdays and two-hour headways on 
weekends but can deviate from the route up to a certain 
distance to pick-up or drop-off patrons. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
FLEX Bus Route 395 
NCTD operates the FLEX Bus Route 395 from Oceanside 
Transit Center north along Stuart Mesa Road, Las Pulgas 
Canyon Road, and Basilone Road to Camp San Onofre. This 
route has a set schedule with approximately three-hour 
headways throughout the week but can deviate from the 
route up to a certain distance to pick-up or drop-off patrons. 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 53 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Rail Service 
Coaster 
NCTD operates the Coaster commuter rail service between 
the Oceanside Transit Center and the Santa Fe Depot in San 
Diego. The route follows the South Coast Highway (101) 
within the City of Oceanside. It operates with 70- to 90-
minute headways on weekday mornings and 30- to 130- 
minutes in the afternoon.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Sprinter 
The Sprinter hybrid-rail system 
connects Oceanside at the Oceanside 
Transit Center to Escondido via the 
Highway 78 corridor. It is operated by 
NCTD. Sprinter has headways of 30-
minutes during most of the day 
throughout the week. On weekends, 
headways are reduced to 60-minutes 
during the early morning and 
evening. 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 54 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Orange County Line 
The Orange County Line is a commuter rail line run by Metrolink from the Oceanside Transit Station 
through Orange County to Union Station in Downtown Los Angeles. The route alignment generally follows 
the Interstate 5 corridor to Union Station. On weekdays, inbound headways are roughly one-hour in the 
morning with limited service in the afternoon and outbound headways are roughly one-hour in the 
evening with limited service in the morning. On weekends, inbound and outbound headways are slightly 
over three-hours. 
 
Inland Empire-Orange County Line 
The Inland Empire–Orange County (IEOC) Line is a commuter rail line run by Metrolink from the 
Oceanside Transit Center through Orange County to San Bernardino. In Oceanside, on weekdays, there is 
a single inbound and single outbound train in the morning and the afternoon with more frequent service 
between Laguna Niguel/Mission Viejo and Downtown Riverside. On weekends, there are two inbound 
trains in the afternoon and two outbound in the morning. 
 
 
Source: Metrolink (2020) 
 
 
 


 
Chapter 4: Transit 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 55 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
Pacific Surfliner 
The Pacific Surfliner is an intercity rail line run by Amtrak between the Santa Fe Depot in Downtown San 
Diego along the Highway 101 corridor to the Oceanside Transit Center and then to the north through 
Orange County, Los Angeles, and Ventura County, terminating in San Luis Obispo. In the City of 
Oceanside, headways are approximately every two to three hours throughout the week and on holidays. 
 
Source: Amtrak (2020)

4.2 
Transit Demand and Ridership 
Commute mode share data was obtained from the US Census 2014 – 2018 American Community Survey 
5-Year Estimates for the City of Oceanside by Census Block Group. Figure 4-2 presents the estimated 
share of the working population who utilize public transportation as their primary commute mode. 
 
The greatest concentration of transit commuters is shown near the Oceanside Transit Center, where 
multiple transit lines converge. An estimate 20.1 – 22% of commuters take public transportation within 
these areas. Additional concentrations are shown north of Mission Avenue near the airport, and 
northeast of the College Boulevard Sprinter station. Ensuring transit services can be safely and 
comfortably accessed via walking and bicycling within these areas is important to sustain and further 
support transit ridership in these areas. 
 
Estimated daily transit ridership by stop is presented in Figure 4-3, representing data obtained from NCTD 
for FY2019. Greater ridership is generally prevalent at both transit centers, the Sprinter stations, and bus 
stops along Mission Avenue which is primarily served by Route 303. 
 
The ten stops with the greatest total estimated daily boardings and alightings are identified in Table 4-1, 
along with the routes serving the locations.

Chapter 4: Transit

Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
4.3 
Transit Quality 
Transit Station Quality 
NCTD’s Bus Stop Development Handbook (2018) identifies four transit stop types: basic stops, bench 
stops, shelter stops, and station stops. Table 4-2 lists the designation criteria, required, recommended, 
and optional transit stop amenities for each stop type.  
 
Table 4-2 Transit Stops Amenity Guidelines  
Stop 
Type 
Designation Criteria 
Required Amenities 
Recommended 
Amenities 
Optional Amenities

Stops 
• Less than 20 daily 
boardings at urban stops 
• Less than 10 daily 
boardings at suburban 
stops 
• Bus stop sign 
• ADA accessible pad 
N/A 
N/A 
Bench 
Stops 
• 20 to 30 daily boardings 
at urban stops 
• 10 to 20 daily boardings 
at suburban stops 
• Bus stop sign 
• ADA accessible pad 
• Bench 
• Connection to adjacent 
sidewalk/pathways 
• Trash receptacle 
• Lighting 
• Bicycle 
racks/lockers 
• Transit Route 
information 
• Shading 
elements 
• Transit system 
information

Stops 
• Greater than 30 daily 
boardings at urban stops 
• Greater than 20 daily 
boardings at suburban 
stops 
• Bus stop sign 
• ADA accessible pad 
• Bench 
• Connection to adjacent

Source: North County Transit District (2020) 
 
An inventory of amenities present at each stop was undertaken to better understand existing stop 
quality. Table 4-3 identifies the amenities provided at each transit stop. The bus stop categories were 
assigned by average daily boardings and urban/suburban designation. Stops west of Interstate 5 were 
categorized as urban, the remaining stops were categorized as suburban. A shaded cell in the table 
indicates a lacking amenity based on the guidelines for the respective stop (Table 4-2). 
 
In 2019, NCTD issued a Staff Report identifying proposed improvements at 100 NCTD bus stops as part of 
an effort to improve transit stop access. Agencies were requested to fund – or identify funding sources – 
construction of the improvements proposed within their respective right-of-way. Table 4-4 lists the 
fourteen bus stops and proposed improvements identified in Oceanside.

Quality Connections from Major Transit Stations 
Quality connections for pedestrians and bicyclists were identified from major transit stops1. The quality 
connections assessment draws from the PEQE and LTS analyses previously presented, to identify quality 
0.25-mile pedestrian and 0.75-mile bicycle networks surrounding each stop. These distances represent a 
five-minute travel distance for pedestrians and cyclists. The total quality walking and cyclist connections 
for each stop was divided by the total available walkable/bikeable street network within a 0.25-mile or 
0.75-mile crow flies buffer from the respective transit stop. The result is a quality walk and quality bike 
ratio for each stop analyzed. 
 
Figure 4-4 displays the results of the quality walkshed ratio. The Oceanside Transit Center and the 
Melrose Drive Station were found to have the greatest quality walk ratios, with 73% and 53% of the 
available network deemed quality, respectively. The Coast Highway Station, El Camino Real Station, and 
College Boulevard Station exhibit lower quality ratios ranging from 13% - 17%. 
 
 
1 The California Governor’s Office of Planning and Research (OPR) defines a major transit stop as “A site containing an existing rail 
transit station, a ferry terminal served by either a bus or rail transit service, or the intersection of two or more major bus routes 
with a frequency of service interval of 15 minutes or less during the morning and afternoon peak commute periods.”

Figure 4-5 presents the quality bikeshed ratios. Similar to the walkshed results, the Oceanside Transit 
Center has the greatest quality bikeshed ratio at 79%. The Rancho Del Oro Station, College Boulevard 
Station, and Melrose Drive Station were all determined to have 0% quality bikesheds. 
 
Service Quality – On-Time Performance 
On-time performance is an important indicator of service quality as it contributes to the users’ perception 
of service reliability. NCTD defines on-time performance as between 59 seconds before and 5 minutes 
and 59 seconds behind the published schedule at the departure location. NCTD has an on-time 
performance standard of 90% for Breeze Bus, 98% for Sprinter, and 95% for Coaster. 
 
Table 4-5 presents the on-time performance by route for the 10 NCTD Breeze Bus routes, Sprinter, and 
Coaster. Two of the bus routes and the Coaster met on-time performance targets. Eight buses missed the 
on-time performance target of 90%. As part of the Mobility Element update, transit signal priority will be 
evaluated along transit corridors which may help increase the on-time performance for bus routes.

4.4 
Safety Near Transit Stops/Stations 
Safety was evaluated using pedestrian- and bicycle-involved collision records within 500 feet of all transit 
stops within the City of Oceanside. Figure 4-6 displays transit stops with pedestrian and/or bicycle 
collisions within 500 feet – stops without records in the vicinity are not depicted. It is important to note 
that some collisions may be double counted if they fall within 500-feet of more than one stop. 
 
Locations with the greatest number of collisions nearby include the following: 
• 
College Boulevard / Oceanside Boulevard 
• 
Crouch Street / Oceanside Boulevard 
• 
San Diego Street / Mission Avenue 
• 
Horne Street / Mission Avenue 
• 
Coast Highway / Topeka Street
