Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 78 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
5.0 Vehicular Mobility 
This chapter describes the key recommendations related to vehicular mobility identified in the currently 
adopted Circulation Element (2012) and regional transportation plan. Vehicular mobility is evaluated 
throughout this chapter in terms of connectivity, demand, quality, and safety. 
 
Maintaining efficient vehicular operations is vital to the economy. City streets and the regional freeway 
system provide an interconnected network used to move the majority of goods and people throughout 
the region. Bus routes, which comprise most public transportation services in Oceanside, also rely on this 
street network.  
 
The Master Transportation Roadway Plan serves as the vehicular chapter of the currently adopted 
Circulation Element. While primarily focused on automobile mobility, the chapter also emphasizes the 
need for a multimodal network of complete streets supported by design guidelines and policies. The 
following two goals guide the chapter: 
• 
A transportation network that supports safe and efficient travel for all modes of transportation. 
• 
A transportation network that is designed to accommodate the existing and future growth of the 
City of Oceanside. 
 
The goals are supported by objectives and policies related to level of service and design standards, and 
future roadway improvements. Table 5-1 presents changes to the circulation network identified in the 
2030 Master Transportation Roadway Plan of the Circulation Element. The proposed improvements will 
be revisited as part of the General Plan Update process to determine if they are still necessary and/or 
desired.

1 Douglas Drive between Via Magaluena and Cardiff Bay Drive will remain as two-lanes. 
2 Widening Lake Boulevard between Thunder Drive and Sundown Lane will accommodate forecast traffic volumes, but it should 
be noted that there are residential dwelling units fronting this segment that oppose the widening. 
3 Melrose Drive between SR-76 and North Santa Fe Avenue could possibly only be a four-lane major instead of a six-lane major in 
2030. 
4 Mission Avenue will be a two-lane one-way couplet between Cleveland and Clementine Streets with Seagaze Drive. Clementine 
Street will be one-way northbound and Cleveland Street will be one-way southbound. 
* Complete. 
 
The current regional transportation plan calls for the expansion of SR-78 to accommodate two managed 
lanes and operational improvements. A total of four managed lanes are planned for I-5 segments. HOV 
and non-HOV connectors are planned for the SR78 – I-5 interchange, while HOV connectors are planned 
for the SR-78 – I-15 interchange. These improvements are currently being reevaluated through the 
ongoing regional transportation plan update, 2021 Regional Plan, which is anticipated to be complete in 
Fall 2021.

5.2 
Operational Evaluation 
Vehicular operational evaluations consist of roadway segment analyses, peak hour arterial analyses, peak 
hour intersection analyses, and peak hour freeway/state highway segment analyses. The City of 
Oceanside jurisdictional limits were used as the general study area boundaries for the vehicular analyses. 
The study area selection for each analysis is further detailed within the respective subsection. 
 
Roadway segment and intersection vehicular operational analyses followed the City of Oceanside Traffic 
Impact Analysis Guidelines for Vehicle Miles Traveled and Level of Service Assessment (2020). Operational 
performance is described in terms of level of Service (LOS), a measure for vehicular operations based on 
traffic flow characteristics. 
 
Table 5-3 describes the characteristics of each LOS category, which range from LOS A – approximating 
free flow conditions, to LOS F – approximating the most congested flow. The quantitative methodologies 
for determining the LOS for each analysis type are described within the respective subsection. 
 
Table 5-3 Vehicular Level of Service Definitions 
LOS 
Characteristics 
A 
Primarily free-flow operation.  Vehicles are completely unimpeded in their ability to maneuver within the traffic 
stream.  Controlled delay at the boundary intersections is minimal.  The travel speed exceeds 85% of the base free-
flow speed. 
B 
Reasonably unimpeded operation.  The ability to maneuver within the traffic stream is only slightly restricted and 
control delay at the boundary intersections is not significant.  The travel speed is between 67% and 85% of the base 
free-flow speed. 
C 
Stable operation.  The ability to maneuver and change lanes at mid-segment locations may be more restricted than 
at LOS B.  Longer queues at the boundary intersections may contribute to lower travel speeds.  The travel speed is 
between 50% and 67% of the base free-flow speed. 
D 
Less stable condition in which small increases in flow may cause substantial increases in delay and decreases in 
travel speed.  This operation may be due to adverse signal progression, high volume, or inappropriate signal timing 
at the boundary intersections.  The travel speed is between 40% and 50% of the base free-flow speed. 
E 
Unstable operation and significant delay.  Such operations may be due to some combination of adverse signal 
progression, high volume, and inappropriate signal timing at the boundary intersections.  The travel speed is 
between 30% and 40% of the base free-flow speed. 
F 
Flow at extremely low speed.  Congestion is likely occurring at the boundary intersections, as indicated by high 
delay and extensive queuing.  The travel speed is 30% or less of the base free-flow speed.  Also, LOS F is assigned 
to the subject direction of travel if the through movement at one or more boundary intersections have a volume-to-
capacity ratio greater than 1.0. 
Source: Highway Capacity Manual, Transportation Research Board (2016) 
 
Methodology for Estimating Traffic Volumes 
Due to the effect that the COVID-19 pandemic has had on travel behavior, this study eschewed collecting 
new traffic counts which are not likely to be reflective of typical conditions.  Instead, traffic volumes were 
estimated from a composite of sources, including historic traffic counts with and without adjustment, and 
Annual Average Daily Trips (AADT) estimated by StreetLight Data, a smart phone data analytics company 
that curates mobility data. The older historic traffic counts underwent validation and adjustment to 
ensure reliability. 


 
Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 95 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
The traffic volumes sourced for this study and any post-processing steps are described below: 
• 
Historic Traffic Counts without Adjustment:  These were counts occurring between 2018 and 
March 2020 collected from the City’s historic count database, traffic impact studies, and from 
data collection firms with historic count databases.  Since these counts occurred within 
approximately the past two years, they were considered reliable and no adjustments were made. 
The count totals were rounded up to the nearest hundred to remain conservative. 
• 
Historic Traffic Counts with Adjustment:  These were counts occurring prior to 2018 or after 
March 2020 collected from the City’s historic count database and from traffic impact studies. 
Since these counts were less reliable, they were adjusted based on the growth rates of adjacent 
roadway segments. The adjusted volumes were also compared to the AADT growth rates 
obtained from StreetLight Data AADT data. 
• 
StreetLight Data AADT Estimates: StreetLight Data mobility data, which estimates traffic through 
smart phone data analytics, was obtained for study area locations lacking traffic count data and 
adjusted using the following process: 
1. Determine the nearest location (to the location lacking traffic counts) with recent traffic 
counts. 
2. Collect StreetLight Data data for the location lacking traffic count as well as the location 
with recent traffic counts.  StreetLight Data data was collected in four different months 
to increase reliability (March, April, September, and October 2019). Location with recent 
traffic counts was inputted into StreetLight Data as a calibration factor. 
3. Obtain the StreetLight Data calculated AADT and validate them against ADT of nearby 
segment. Further refinements such as trips balancing to account for high trip generation 
area such as a shopping center  
 
Peak hour intersections volumes were developed using the same process, with further refinement using 
StreetLight Data data filtered by AM and PM peak hour time periods and with volume balancing between 
closely spaced intersections. Table 5-4 summarizes the quantity of study area roadway segments and 
intersections by each volume source. 
 
Table 5-4 Vehicular Count Data Sources 
Count Source 
Roadway 
Segment Counts 
Intersection 
Counts

58

Source: CR Associates (2020)

All Circulation Element roadway segments that meet one or more of the following conditions were 
analyzed:

A total of 139 roadway segments were evaluated. 
 
Roadway segment LOS categories are based on theoretical daily vehicular capacity thresholds, which are 
influenced by a roadway’s functional classification and its lane and median geometry. Existing or 
forecasted average daily traffic (ADT) volumes are compared to these thresholds to determine the LOS of 
a roadway segment.  Table 5-5 presents the roadway segment capacity thresholds, which are based on 
the City of Oceanside’s traffic impact analysis guidelines. LOS A through D operations are regarded as 
acceptable performance for Mobility Element roadway segments in the City of Oceanside.

These standards are generally used as long-range planning guidelines to determine the functional 
classification of roadways. The actual capacity of a roadway facility varies according to its physical and 
operational attributes. Often, a roadway segment that is analyzed to be LOS E or F based on theoretical 
capacity is found to operate acceptably in practice or vice versa. In such cases, HCM arterial analysis may 


 
Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 97 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
be conducted and utilized (or intersection analysis, if arterial analysis is not applicable) to provide a more 
accurate indication of LOS.

level of services. Table 5-6 identifies the functional classification, substandard volume threshold, average 
daily traffic volume, volume to capacity ratio, and resulting level of service for each study area roadway 
segment.

Peak-Hour Arterial Analysis 
As shown in Table 5-6, roadway analysis using the City of Oceanside Circulation Element Roadway 
Classification LOS & Vehicular Capacity determined that SR-76 operate at acceptable LOS D or better.  
However, based on the ground experiences and review of historical data, SR-76 typically experiences 
congestion during many peak hours of the day. As alluded to in the previous section, in this case, a peak 
hour arterial analysis is necessary to provide a more accurate indication of LOS along SR-76. 
 
Peak hour arterial analyses were conducted along SR-76. The average travel speed is computed from the 
running time on the arterial segments and the intersection approach delay. Average speed is strongly 
influenced by the number of signals per mile and the average intersection delay. On a given facility, 
factors such as inappropriate signal timing, poor progression, and increasing traffic flow can substantially 
degrade the arterial level of service. 
 
Table 5-7 shows the LOS thresholds used for the arterial speed analysis as identified in Caltrans’ Highway 
Capacity Manual. The computerized analysis of arterial speed was performed with the Synchro 10.0 
(Trafficware, 2020) traffic analysis software. 
 
Table 5-7 Arterial Analysis Level of Service Thresholds

Peak-Hour Intersection Level of Service 
Signalized Intersection Analysis Methodology 
The signalized intersection analysis utilized in this study conforms to the operational analysis 
methodology outlined in 2016 Highway Capacity Manual (HCM 6). This method defines LOS in terms of 
delay, or more specifically, average control delay per vehicle (seconds/vehicle). 
 
The HCM 6 methodology sets 1,900 passenger-cars per hour per lane (pcphpl) as the ideal saturation flow 
rate at signalized intersections based upon the minimum headway that can be sustained between 
departing vehicles at a signalized intersection. The service saturation flow rate, which reflects the 
saturation flow rate specific to the study facility, is determined by adjusting the ideal saturation flow rate 
for lane width, on-street parking, bus stops, pedestrian volume, traffic composition (or percentage of 
heavy vehicles), and shared lane movements (e.g. through and right-turn movements sharing the same 
lane). The computerized analysis of intersection operations was performed utilizing the Synchro 10.0 
(Trafficware, 2020) traffic analysis software. 
 
The following assumptions were utilized in conducting all intersection level of service analyses: 
• 
Each signalized intersection assumed 10 pedestrian crossing signal activations per leg/signal 
phase per hour, an estimate that is more conservative than prevailing HCM methodology 
• 
A 2% heavy vehicle factor was applied for all study area intersections. 2% is a standard 
assumption in HCM methodologies 
• 
Application of a Peak Hour Factor (PHF): Obtained from existing peak hour counts if available or a 
default 0.92 PHF was used. 0.92, as provided in HCM. 
• 
Signal Timing obtained from existing signal timing plans (as of June 2020), included as Appendix E 


 
Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 108 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
Table 5-9 presents the signalized intersection average control delay per vehicle thresholds and describes 
the operational characteristics of each LOS category. 
 
Table 5-9 Signalized Intersection Level of Service Operations Thresholds and Characteristics 
Level of Service 
Average Control 
Delay Per Vehicle 
(seconds) 
Level of Service Characteristics 
A 
<10.0 
Occurs when the volume-to-capacity ratio is low and either progression is exceptionally 
favorable or the cycle length is very short. If it is due to favorable progression, most 
vehicles arrive during the green indication and travel through the intersection without 
stopping. 
B 
10.1 – 20.0 
Occurs when the volume-to-capacity ratio is low and either progression is highly 
favorable or the cycle length is short. More vehicles stop than with LOS A. 
C 
20.1 – 35.0 
Occurs when progression is favorable or the cycle length is moderate. The number of 
vehicles stopping is significant, although many vehicles still pass through the intersection 
without stopping. 
D 
35.1 – 55.0 
Occurs when the volume-to-capacity ratio is high and either progression is ineffective or 
the cycle length is long. Many vehicles stop and individual cycle failures are noticeable. 
E 
55.1 – 80.0 
Occurs when the volume-to-capacity ratio is high, progression is unfavorable, and the 
cycle length is long. Individual cycle failures are frequent. 
F 
>80.0 
Occurs when the volume-to-capacity ratio is very high, progression is very poor, and the 
cycle length is long. Most cycles fail to clear the queue. 
Source: Highway Capacity Manual, Transportation Research Board (2016) 
 
Unsignalized Intersection Analysis Methodology 
Unsignalized intersections, including two-way and all-way stop controlled intersections, were analyzed 
using the HCM 6 unsignalized intersection analysis methodology, also utilizing Synchro 10.0 traffic analysis 
software. The LOS for a side street stop controlled (SSSC) intersection is determined by the computed or 
measured control delay and is defined for each minor movement. The LOS for an all-way stop controlled 
(AWSC) intersection is determined by the computed or measured average control delay of all 
movements. Table 5-10 presents the level of service operations thresholds for unsignalized intersections. 
LOS A through D operations are regarded as acceptable performance for Mobility Element intersections 
in the City of Oceanside. 
 
Table 5-10 Unignalized Intersection Level of Service Operations Thresholds and Characteristics 
Level of Service 
Average Control Delay Per Vehicle (seconds) 
A 
<10.0 
B 
10.1 – 15.0 
C 
15.1 – 25.0 
D 
25.1 – 35.0 
E 
35.1 – 50.0 
F 
>50.0 
Source: Highway Capacity Manual, Transportation Research Board (2016)

Notes: 
Bold letter indicates LOS E or F. 
1 Uncontrolled intersection – no delay experienced by any of the approaches. 
SSSC = Side-Street Stop Controlled. SSYC = Side-Street Yield Controlled. 
For SSSC and SSYC intersections, the delay shown is the worst delay experienced by any of the movements. 
 
Freeway/Highway Level of Service 
Freeway level of service analysis is based upon procedures developed by Highway Capacity Manual 2010. 
The procedure for calculating freeway LOS involves estimating the vehicle speed (mi/h) and density/flow 
(pc/mi/ln).   
 
HCS7 software, developed by McTrans, was used to calculate both the vehicle speed and density/flow 
along the study area freeway segments. The HCS7 software required the following inputs to complete the 
speed and density/flow calculations: 
• 
AADT – Caltrans Traffic Census 2018 AADT Volumes Report 
• 
K (peak hour percentage) – Caltrans Traffic Census 2018 AADT Volumes Report 
• 
D (directional split) – Caltrans Traffic Census 2018 AADT Volumes Report 
• 
AADTadj – Calculated using AADT and D values provided by Caltrans using the following 
equation: 
o AADTadj = (
𝐷𝐷
1−𝐷𝐷)AADT  
 
o represents the direction in the opposite direction of the Peak Direction 
• 
PHF – Assumed to be a typical value of 0.95 
• 
PT (% Trucks and Busses) – Caltrans Traffic Census 2018 AADT Truck Volumes Report 
• 
PR (% RVs) – Assumed to be 0, HCM 6 recommends grouping RV volumes with Trucks in Buses 
as the value is assumed less than a 5:1 ratio 
• 
General Terrain – Assumed to be less than 2% grade and therefore Level Terrain (HCM 6 11-
16,17) 
• 
fp – Driver population factor assumed one as traffic is largely commuter traffic 
• 
ET – Value of 1.5 as terrain is Level (HCM 6 11-15) 
• 
ER – Value of 1.2 as terrain is Level (HCM 6 11-15) 


 
Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 116 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
• 
Lane Width – Assumed 12’ maximum value by Google Earth survey 
• 
Rt-Side Lat. Clearance – Assumed 6’ maximum value by Google Earth survey 
• 
Total Ramp Density, TRD – Calculated based on Google Earth survey 
o Density calculated by total number of on/off ramps in single direction within segment 
length plus 3 miles in both directions, divided by the total length 
• 
Base free-flow Speed, BFFS – Assumed 75.4 mph (HCM 6 11-11) 
 
Using the calculated freeway speed and 
density/flow, the LOS is determined using the 
chart to the right.  
 
Two freeways serve the City of Oceanside, 
Interstate 5 and State Route 78, carrying 
significant volumes of traffic while providing 
regional connections. 
 
Interstate 5 
Interstate 5 (I-5) is a north-south facility 
connecting San Diego County to the US-Mexico 
International Border to the south and Orange 
County to the north. The freeway is maintained 
and operated by Caltrans. Within Oceanside, I-5 
has four mixed-flow/general purpose lanes and one auxiliary lane in each direction. Access to I-5 within 
Oceanside is provided via Harbor Drive, SR-76, Mission Avenue, Oceanside Boulevard, California Street 
(northbound only), Cassidy Street (southbound only), and eastbound Vista Way/westbound SR-78. In 
2018, I-5 carried between 171,000 and 210,000 annual average daily traffic (AADT) along segments within 
Oceanside. Heavy vehicle traffic was estimated to account for 4.8% to 6.0% of total daily traffic. 
 
State Route 78 
State Route 78 (SR-78) is an east-west facility spanning between I-5 in Oceanside to the west to I-10 in 
the City of Blythe near the California-Arizona State Border. The facility is maintained and operated by 
Caltrans. Within Oceanside, SR-78 has two to three westbound and two to four eastbound mixed-
flow/general purpose lanes and one auxiliary lane in each direction. Access to SR-78 within Oceanside is 
provided via eastbound Vista Way, I-5 interchange, Jefferson Street, El Camino Real, and College 
Boulevard. In 2018, SR-78 carried between 120,000 to 135,000 AADT along segments within Oceanside. 
Heavy vehicle traffic was estimated to account for 4.7% to 4.9% of total daily traffic. 
 
Table 5-12 presents freeway level of service analysis results for I-5 and SR-78 segments within the vicinity 
of Oceanside. The Caltrans data is representative of year 2018 and is provided in Appendix G. 
 
As shown, the following segments currently operate at a substandard level of service: 
• 
I-5 NB, from Oceanside Boulevard to California Street – AM (LOS E) 
• 
I-5 NB, from California Street to Cassidy Street – AM (LOS E) 
• 
I-5 NB, from Cassidy Street to Vista Way/SR-78 – AM (LOS E) 
• 
I-5 NB, from Vista Way/SR-78 to Southern City Limits – AM (LOS E) 
• 
SR-78 EB, from I-5 to Jefferson Street – PM (LOS E) 
Source: Highway Capacity Manual (2010)

5.3 
Vehicular Safety 
Vehicular collision data was obtained from the Oceanside Police Department for the 5-year period 2015 – 
2019. A total of 6,861 vehicular collisions were reported, excluding those involving a pedestrian or 
bicyclist, or occurring on I-5 or SR-78. Figure 5-6 displays vehicular collisions by year. Annual vehicular 
collisions ranged between a low of 1,108 collisions in 2018 and peaked in 2016 with 1,639 collisions.  
 
Figure 5-6 
Vehicular Collisions by Year (2015 – 2019) 
 
Source: Oceanside Police Department (2020) 
 
Figure 5-7 displays vehicular collisions across the City. Collisions are largely concentrated at intersections 
along major roads, such as SR-76, Oceanside Boulevard, El Camino Real, and College Boulevard. As 
previously shown in Figure 5-3, these corridors carry the City’s greatest volumes of vehicular traffic. 
Concentrations are also shown west of I-5 and along Coast Highway, which has a dense street network 
with closely spaced intersections, when compared to the more suburban development east of I-5. 
 
Table 5-13 identifies the 10 intersections with the greatest vehicular collision frequency. Five of the 10 
intersections are located along SR-76, while College Boulevard and El Camino Real each contain three of 
the high frequency collision intersections.

Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility

Chapter 5: Vehicular Mobility 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 121 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
Table 5-14 displays vehicular collisions by the reported crash type. The leading crash types were rear end, 
sideswipe, and hit object. When focusing on only collisions involving an injury or fatality (2,603 
combined), the leading crash types are overwhelmingly rear end (992 collisions) and broadside (660 
collisions) crash types.  
 
Table 5-14 Vehicle Collisions by Crash Type (2015 – 2019) 
Crash Type 
Number of Collisions 
Rear End 
2,049 
Sideswipe 
1,479 
Hit Object 
1,100 
Broadside 
953 
Head On 
387 
Other 
280 
Overturned 
120 
Blank 
493 
Total 
6,861 
Source: Oceanside Police Department (2020) 
 
Table 5-15 identifies the ten most frequent violation codes reported for vehicular collisions. The leading 
violation code cited was due to vehicles speeding, which accounts for nearly three times as many records 
as any other violation. The second most frequently reported violation code for vehicular collisions was 
due to drivers being under the influence of alcohol. Both violations are the result of improper driver 
behaviors. Combined, the 2,334 collisions account for more than a third of total vehicle collisions in 
Oceanside. Improving driver behaviors through enforcement or educational strategies targeted at these 
topics may help reduce vehicular collisions.

Table 5-15 Leading Vehicle Collision Violation Codes (2015 – 2019) 
 
Violation 
Code 
Description 
Number of 
Collisions 
MISC-HAZ 
Miscellaneous Hazard 
1,726 
22350 
No person shall drive a vehicle upon a highway at a speed greater than is reasonable or 
prudent having due regard for weather, visibility, the traffic on, and the surface and width of, 
the highway, and in no event at a speed which endangers the safety of persons or property. 
1,651 
23152(a) 
It is unlawful for a person who is under the influence of any alcoholic beverage to drive a 
vehicle. 
683 
22107

Rankings from the California Office of Traffic Safety (OTS) are a supplemental data source used to 
understand the magnitude of collisions within a city. The OTS rankings compare collision data between 
cities of similar size. The ranking methodology assigns weights to different statistical categories including 
observed crash counts, population, and vehicle miles traveled. The OTS data only includes collisions 
resulting in an injury or fatality, whereas previous collision data presented throughout this report also 
include property damage only collisions. The rankings should be used for high-level comparisons to 
indicate potential challenges. There are many other factors unique to each city that could influence the 
ranking yet are not accounted for. Additional details on the OTS crash ranking methodology and inputs 
can be found on the OTS website: https://www.ots.ca.gov/media-and-research/crash-rankings/ 
 
The City of Oceanside falls in OTS Group B which includes 59 cities with populations of 100,001 – 250,000 
residents. Additional cities in San Diego County falling within Group B include Carlsbad, Vista, and

Escondido. A ranking of 1 indicates the city with the worst results. For Group B, a ranking of 59 is the best 
possible, meaning it performed better than the other 58 cities in the group. Table 5-16 presents the 2018 
OTS results as well as the other Group B cities within San Diego County. The rankings indicate Oceanside 
experiences  higher number of collisions involving motorcycles, alcohol involvement with drivers under 
age 21, and speed related collisions when compared to other cities in Group B.   Pedestrian-involved 
collisions and bicycle-involved collisions with cyclists under age 15 are relatively lower among Group B 
cities.

Chapter 6: Intelligent Transportation Systems

6.0 Intelligent Transportation Systems 
Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITS) encompass a broad range of technologies and associated 
operations that seek to improve and maximize the existing transportation infrastructure's efficiency. 
When integrated into the transportation system's infrastructure and in vehicles themselves, these 
technologies can alleviate congestion, improve safety, and enhance productivity. It is also a cost-effective 
means of increasing the efficiency of the transportation system. 
 
The use of ITS to improve our transportation resources and networks' efficiency and safety has been an 
increasing trend. It highlights the need to make the most of the physical infrastructure and reduce 
impacts to the overall urban environment. While the focus is frequently on the technologies being 
deployed through ITS, it is important to note that each ITS effort must be supported by appropriate 
operations and maintenance resources to ensure long-term effectiveness. 
 
The currently adopted Circulation Element identifies the following two ITS-specific goals: 
• 
Encourage and support the use of Intelligent Transportation System technologies to increase the 
efficiency of the transportation network. 
• 
Support the use of Intelligent Transportation System technologies as a cost-effective alternative 
to improve the transportation network. 
 
The objectives and policies supporting the ITS goals identify specific technologies to pursue, such as 
Advanced Traffic Management System, fiber optic cables, changeable electronic message signs, and CCTV 
camera systems. 
 
The City of Oceanside utilizes transportation management technologies to analyze and manage 
congestion throughout the City. The City currently has 162 signalized intersections, which are managed 
through a combination of ITS technologies as documented throughout this section. 
 
Transportation Management Center 
The City of Oceanside’s Transportation Management Center (TMC) is located at the City Hall, where the 
City's Emergency Operations Center (EOC) is also housed. The TMC is operated and maintained by the 
Transportation Engineering Section. Out of the 161 signalized intersections within the City of Oceanside, 
approximately 96% of the signals are connected to the TMC. 
 
The TMC provides the City's staff with comprehensive communication infrastructure to coordinate 
transportation management at local and regional levels. The TMC uses a central network and video walls 
connected to the City's traffic signal communication system to monitor real-time traffic flows and make 
adjustments, as needed.  
 
Advanced Traffic Management System 
The City of Oceanside currently uses a advanced traffic management system called Transparity Traffic 
Management System by McCain, Inc. This system allows City staff at the TMC to remotely survey traffic 
conditions, monitor system-wide signal status in real-time, alert staff of any abnormal conditions, manage 
signal timing, and analyze traffic trends.  


 
Chapter 6: Intelligent Transportation Systems 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 125 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
Closed-Circuit Television (CCTV) 
Approximately 40 CCTVs are installed throughout the City, providing live traffic conditions back to the 
TMC. The purpose of the CCTV is to allow City staff to monitor and adjust signal timing to meet traffic 
demands. 
 
Planned Adaptive Signal Control Technology 
Adaptive signal control technology is an advanced signal control technology that adjusts the green time of 
a signal based on volume and existing conditions. Unlike conventional signal systems where signal timing 
(red/yellow/green) is based on a pre-programed daily signal timing schedules, adaptive signals can 
conduct live traffic counts and adjust the signal timing accordingly. The adaptive signal's benefit is that it 
can continuously distribute green time equitably for all traffic movements, improve travel time reliability 
by progressively moving vehicles through green lights, and reduce congestion by creating a smoother 
traffic flow. The City of Oceanside currently does not have an adaptive signal system, however, the City 
anticipates Caltrans installing adaptive signals at seven intersections along SR-76. 
 
Arterial Speed and Conditions Detection 
The City of Oceanside utilizes various systems to monitor arterial speeds and queues similar to the 
specialized detection in place along much of the region's freeway network. Devices are placed in or 
alongside the roadbed to detect vehicles as they pass, counting the number of vehicles and speeds during 
a unit of time (typically 60 seconds). This information can be used for monitoring traffic conditions by 
staff at the TMC, reporting information to traveler information systems such as 511, and long-term 
monitoring of traffic speeds and trends. The City of Oceanside currently has or plans to install the 
following technologies: 
BlueTOAD by TrafficCast:  BlueTOAD utilizes Bluetooth to measure travel times, travel patterns, 
and congestion. BlueTOAD detects the Bluetooth Media Access Control Address (MAC Address) 
of Bluetooth equipped vehicle or Bluetooth equipped devices throughout the transportation 
network and compares them to determine the traffic condition. There are currently 14 BlueTOAD 
devices installed throughout the City of Oceanside, located along Coast Highway, Mission Avenue, 
Oceanside Boulevard, Vista Way, and College Boulevard. 
Blyncsy Pulse: Caltrans is planning to install seven Blyncsy Pulse detectors along SR-76 as a part of 
the adaptive signal upgrade, in addition to the three already present. Blyncsy takes traffic flow 
data at each intersection, combines them with big data to monitor real-time traffic, analyze 
historical trends, identify patterns, and alert staff at the TMC if any abnormal conditions occur. 
This data will be provided to the City of Oceanside. 
 
Table 6-1 identifies the number of current and planned signals within the City of Oceanside, as well as the 
number of signals with ITS technologies identified above.

7.0 Transportation Demand Management 
Transportation Demand Management (TDM) refers to various programs and strategies intended to 
encourage alternative transportation choices with the goal of reducing vehicular traffic during peak 
commute hours. Infrastructure that supports pedestrian and bicycle mobility is one method to encourage 
trips by these modes that is largely within the City’s control. Similarly, a robust and efficient public transit 
network can help reduce single occupant vehicle trips, however this generally requires coordination with 
the transit provider (NCTD) and/or regional planning organization (SANDAG). 
 
The currently adopted Circulation Element identifies one TDM-specific goal, supported by objectives and 
policies. The goal reads as follows: 
• 
Support programs that encourage increased vehicle occupancies and trip reduction in order for 
residents to enjoy the quality of life that currently exists in Oceanside. 
 
The objectives and supporting policies are generally related to increasing transit, walking, bicycling, and 
teleworking use. 
 
The City of Oceanside, residents, employees, and employers all have access to the regional TDM program 
iCommute. SANDAG administers iCommute due to the regional significance and common interest in 
reducing peak commute hour trips. The following programs and services are offered: 
• 
Employer Services Program – Free assistance to local business to help them develop and 
implement employee commuter benefit programs that lower costs, increase productivity, and 
help the environment. 
• 
Vanpool Program – SANDAG contracts with vanpool vendors that provide vehicles, maintenance, 
and insurance. SANDAG also provides up to a $400 monthly subsidy to qualified vanpools. 
• 
Guaranteed Ride Home (GRH) – Serves as a safety net for commuters who carpool, vanpool, ride 
transit, walk, or bike to work three or more times per work. GRH provides a free taxi or rideshare 
service ride up to three times per year in the event of a family emergency, unscheduled overtime, 
or being stranded from a carpool or vanpool, or bicycle issue. 
• 
Bike Encouragement Program – Supports bike commuting by providing Bike Month and Bike to 
Work Day events, and the San Diego Regional Bike Map. iCommute manages more than 800 bike 
lockers at more than 60 transit stations and Park & Ride lots throughout San Diego County. 
• 
Walk, Ride, and Roll to School – Education and outreach program to increase the number of 
students who walk, bike, skate, or ride a scooter to school. 
• 
Carpool Match – iCommute provides access to a database of commuters looking for a carpool 
match. 
• 
Park & Ride Map – Map identifying the location of approximately 90 Park & Ride lots in the San 
Diego Region and southern Riverside County. Oceanside currently has  
 
iCommute program offerings are marketed through a variety of promotional campaigns, such as Bike 
Month and Rideshare Month. The iCommute website (www.iCommuteSD.com) provides links to 


 
Chapter 7: Transportation Demand Management 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 128 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
additional resources and information intended to encourage alternatives to single occupant vehicle 
commutes. 
 
The City of Oceanside’s CAP cites TDM programs as one of the measures to help meet future VMT 
reduction goals. The following strategies are identified in the CAP: 
• 
Adopt a TDM Ordinance that requires new commercial and industrial developments, or 
commercial and industrial developments that undergo a permitted renovation affecting more 
than 10,000 square feet, that would generate more than 100 vehicle commute trips per day to 
prepare a TDM Plan. The City shall prepare and maintain a checklist of common TDM measures to 
streamline preparation of TDM plans. 
• 
Conduct surveys of business to determine existing TDM measure implementation rates. Require 
businesses to complete surveys during business license renewal. Provide rebates for 
implementation of TDM measures such as addition of bicycle parking or transit stop shelter 
improvements. 
• 
Provide incentives and marketing promotion through Green Oceanside Business Network for 
businesses that incorporate TDM measures.

8.0 Airport and Goods Movement 
8.1 
Airport 
The Oceanside Municipal Airport is located in north central Oceanside, just north of SR-76 between Benet 
Road and Foussat Road. The 43-acre general aviation airport features a dual runway, aviation fuel, 
transient parking, and hangar rentals. Sixty-four aircraft are based on the field, including 61 airplanes and 
three helicopters. 
 
During the 12-month period from August 2018 – August 2019, daily operations averaged 76 aircraft per 
day. The aircraft were split between 51% transient general aviation and 49% local general aviation 
(www.airnav.com). 
 
Future development of the airport is guided by the Airport Master Plan, adopted in 1994. The City 
initiated work to update the long-range planning document in 2012, however, it currently remains in 
draft form until funding is secured to prepare the accompanying environmental documentation. The draft 
document anticipates the site accommodating a minimum of 90 airplanes, and forecasts approximately 
24,300 annual takeoffs and landings. Additional hangars, a terminal building expansion, and a designated 
viewing area are also identified in the draft plan. 
 
8.2 
Goods Movement 
The efficient movement of goods is essential sustaining the local economy and meeting basic consumer 
needs. Freight travels via a vast interconnected multimodal transportation network consisting of 
roadways, railways, maritime facilities, and airports. 
 
Within Oceanside, goods are moved through the roadway network, primarily along I-5, SR-78, SR-76, and 
the arterials that provide access to commercial and industrial businesses. Trucks depend on efficient 
operations with streets designed to accommodate wide turning radii and significant weight to efficiently 
reach their destinations. Goods are also delivered directly to consumers on smaller vehicles via the US 
Postal Service and private carriers such as UPS, FedEx, and Amazon. 
 
The City of Oceanside does not have an adopted Truck Route map, however, Traffic Code Section 12.3 
prohibits heavy vehicles – defined as vehicles of a gross weight more than 10,000 pounds – from using 
the following street segments: 
• 
Cassidy Street between Hill Street and Hunsaker Street 
• 
Crouch Street between Grandview and Oceanside Boulevard and between Mesa Drive and 
Canyon Drive 
• 
Mesa Drive between Mission Avenue and North Santa Fe 
• 
Cameo Drive/Marvin between College Boulevard and Rancho del Oro 
• 
Rancho del Oro between Oceanside Boulevard and Vista Way and between Mission Avenue and 
Mesa Drive 
• 
Foussat Drive between Mission Avenue and Oceanside Boulevard 
• 
Ivy Road between Vista Way and Via Esmarca 
• 
Old Grove Road/Temple Heights Drive between College Boulevard and Oceanside Boulevard 
• 
Avocado/Ivy between Fire Mountain and Foussat Drive 


 
Chapter 8: Airport and Goods Movement 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update

Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
• 
South Vista Way west of Jefferson Street 
• 
Carolyn Circle between Mission Avenue and Foussat Drive 
• 
San Luis Rey Road aka North El Camino Real between Douglas Drive and Peyri Road 
• 
Autumn Drive/Springtime Drive/Winter Road between Olive Avenue and North Avenue 
• 
Seasons Road/Summer Drive/Fall Place between Olive Avenue and North Avenue 
•

•

•

•

• 
Glenhaven Drive between Meadowbrook Drive and Temple Heights Drive 
 
In addition to truck traffic, rail freight passes through Oceanside along the western community boundary, 
operated by the Burlington Northern Santa Fe Railway Company (BNSF). Although the freight does not 
stop in Oceanside, BNSF operates freight rail service along the same right-of-way as the Amtrak and 
Coaster, making it an important planning consideration. The LOSSAN Corridor Strategic Assessment (2010) 
anticipates freight rail frequencies within the corridor to double from 4 freight trans per day to 8 over the 
next 10 years.

City of Oceanside General Plan Update

Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
9.0 Planning Issues and Implications 
This chapter provides a summary of the key issues and opportunities to consider in the future project 
phases. 
 
9.1 
Pedestrian 
The areas exhibiting the greatest levels of propensity for pedestrian and bicycle trips include west of I-5 
around Downtown, the western part of Loma Alta, the southeastern parts of the Tri-City neighborhood, 
northwest of the Melrose Drive Sprinter station, and the areas to the west and to the east of the San Luis 
Rey Transit Center. 
 
Focusing improvements in these areas that enhance pedestrian safety, comfort, and convenience should 
be considered. In addition to having relatively greater activity levels, these areas also exhibit relatively 
higher frequencies of pedestrian-involved collisions. Drivers failing to yield to pedestrians was the leading 
violation attributed to pedestrian-involved collisions. Intersection crossing treatments such as high 
visibility crosswalks, curb extensions, pedestrian countdown signal heads with lead pedestrian intervals 
can help improve the visibility of pedestrians to drivers while also prioritizing pedestrians and make their 
trips more convenient. 
 
Figure 9-1 summarizes findings from the pedestrian analysis, identifying locations with higher propensity 
for active transportation trips, higher pedestrian collision frequencies, major roadways with 
environments uninviting to pedestrians, and planned improvements intended to create more pedestrian 
friendly environments. 
 
9.2 
Bicycle 
The San Luis Rey River Trail, partially complete Coastal Rail Trail, and future Inland Rail Trail are critical 
links to the City and regional bike network. These facilities are intended to provide comfortable bicycling 
connections for users of all skill levels that are separated from vehicular traffic. 
 
Completing these facilities should be a priority for improving bicycling connectivity and safety. The 
benefits of these investments can be maximized by ensuring safe and comfortable connections from 
nearby neighborhoods and attention to any at-grade street crossings. Ensuring bicyclists can comfortably 
access key activity centers and destinations from these facilities is critical to attracting new riders. 
 
The main arterials connecting residential neighborhoods to destinations are generally striped with Class II 
bike lanes, however, these are still environments uncomfortable or uninviting to most bicyclists. 
 
Improving bicyclist comfort along arterials is difficult, due to the high speeds and constrained right-of-way 
in many locations. Using Level of Traffic Stress methodology, posted speed limits greater than 35 mph 
require a physical protected facility, such as a bike path or protected bike lane, to make conditions 
comfortable for a greater segment of the bicycling population. Where practical, alternative parallel 
connections should be considered, such as the Inland Rail Trail as a parallel alternative to Oceanside 
Boulevard.

Intersection approaches are critical considerations for arterials when protected facilities are infeasible. 
Continuing the bicycle lane up to the intersection can help bicyclists navigate through these high stress 
environments and facilitate predictable bicycling behaviors and positioning. Green paint at conflict points 
and additional markings can further remind drivers to anticipate bicyclists. 
 
The bicycle analysis findings are summarized in Figure 9-2, identifying areas with higher active 
transportation propensity, areas with higher bicycle collision frequency, the regional Class I facilities, and 
roadways exhibiting environments that are stressful for bicyclists. 
 
9.3 
Transit 
The Oceanside Transit Center serves as a major regional transit hub, with Breeze Bus, Coaster, Sprinter, 
Amtrak, and Metrolink services all offered. It is the only location in the region where all of these services 
converge. 
 
The Transit Center also falls within one of the areas with the greatest propensity for active transportation 
within the City. Future considerations should be made to enhance pedestrian and bicycle access around 
the Transit Center, as a means to strengthen first-/last-mile connections and further encourage use of the 
vast transit offerings and multimodal trips. The anticipated redevelopment of the Transit Center is an 
opportunity to leverage these services and improve multimodal access while creating a destination. 
 
The Sprinter is an exceptional investment that should be leveraged through more transit-supportive land 
uses. 
 
The Sprinter provides a high-quality hybrid-rail system connecting the Oceanside Transit Center to 
Escondido, with seven stops in Oceanside. However, ridership is low at many stops due to lack of 
surrounding land uses. Focusing future development at or near the Sprinter stations, coupled with strong 
pedestrian and bicycle connections, is one strategy the City should consider to encourage future public 
transit use. 
 
Figure 9-3 displays transit routes, 0.25-mile walksheds around transit stops, high ridership transit stops, 
and transit stops with high frequencies of pedestrian and bicycle collisions in the vicinity. 
 
9.4 
Vehicular 
Planned improvements at the regional level will improve operations along the freeways and encourage 
carpooling. 
 
The current regional transportation plan calls for the expansion of SR-78 to accommodate two managed 
lanes and operational improvements. A total of four managed lanes are planned for I-5 segments. HOV 
and non-HOV connectors are planned for the SR78 – I-5 interchange, while HOV connectors are planned 
for the SR-78 – I-15 interchange. These improvements are currently being reevaluated through the 
ongoing regional transportation plan update, San Diego Forward, which is anticipated to be complete in 
2021. Additionally, SANDAG will be initiating the North County Regional Corridor Comprehensive 
Multimodal Corridor Plan in 2021 to improve multimodal mobility between I-5 and I-15 in North County. 
 


 
Chapter 9: Planning Issues and Implications 
 
City of Oceanside General Plan Update 
 
 Page 134 
Mobility Existing Conditions Report 
 
 
 
The built-out environment prevalent throughout most of Oceanside limits opportunities for roadway 
expansions, emphasizing the importance of utilizing technology to improve operations and encouraging 
alternative transportation modes. 
 
The City should continue to invest in intersection technology enhancements as the primary method of 
improving vehicular flow. Widening streets is generally not supported by community members and can be 
detrimental to the safety and mobility of alternative transportation modes. Instead, the City should 
continue its investment in alternative transportation modes and strengthen first-/last-mile connections to 
major transit stops. Future development should also be focused within these “smart growth” areas to 
further encourage and facilitate trips via public transit, walking, and bicycling. Additionally, traffic calming 
measures should continue to be explored throughout the City as a means to improve vehicular safety and 
help reduce severe/fatal collisions.  
 
Figure 9-4 summarizes the analysis results, displaying vehicular segments and intersections currently 
operating at level of service D, E, or F, high collisions locations, and the planned improvement area 
encompassed by the Coast Highway Vision and Strategic Plan.
